->**Hermetic Philosophy**<-
<note>original Latin: HERM. PHILOSOPHIA. METICA. This text appears within a circular bookplate. The term <term>Hermetic Philosophy</term> refers to a tradition of esoteric wisdom, alchemy, and spiritual transformation rooted in the writings of the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

<note>The bookplate depicts a white double-headed eagle on a blue field, holding a red four-petaled cross. While the eagle is a common imperial symbol, in alchemical imagery, it often represents the "volatile" spirit or the process of sublimation.</note>

**A. Wienner von Sonnenfels**
<note>This is the signature of Aloys Wienner von Sonnenfels (1705–1768). Born Lipmann Perlin, he was a Jewish scholar of Hebrew and Oriental languages who converted to Christianity. He became a professor and a court interpreter in Vienna. He was well-known for his deep interest in alchemy and the Hermetic arts, making this manuscript part of his personal intellectual collection.</note>

# ASSERTIONS
### CONCERNING THE ONE AND TRIUNE GOD, THE INCARNATION OF THE WORD, THE GRACE OF THE SAVIOR, AND THE THEOLOGICAL VIRTUES.

->I.<-

The first truth of Theology is that GOD exists; it is so clear that it cannot be long ignored; GOD is pure Spirit, infinite in every kind of perfection.

**II.** In GOD there is a fullness of all knowledge, and specifically, besides the knowledge of simple intelligence and vision, the knowledge of conditionals <note>original: "scientia conditionalium." This refers to "middle knowledge" regarding how free creatures would act in certain circumstances.</note> according to the Suarezian system. <note>Named after Francisco Suárez (1548–1617), a highly influential Jesuit theologian whose work shaped early modern Catholic thought.</note>

**III.** Pre-defining decrees <note>Divine decrees regarding the future.</note> do not hinder free will, if they are made in consultation with human freedom; however, they would hinder it if they were made without such consultation.

**IV.** GOD wills all men to be saved: Election to glory is made based on the foresight of merits, just as reprobation is made after merits are absolutely foreseen.

**V.** Against the Socinians <note>A sect that denied the Trinity and the divinity of Christ.</note>, the faith of the Most Holy Trinity is shown from the scriptures and tradition. Against the Arians, we demonstrate the divinity of the Son, and against the Macedonians, the divinity of the Holy Spirit: The Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son.

**VI.** To strengthen faith in the Incarnation, we take invincible arguments from Prophecies, miracles, and the astounding propagation of the Christian Religion: specifically against the Jews, we show the coming of the Messiah from the Prophecy of Jacob. <note>Referring to Genesis 49:10, a traditional text used to argue that the Messiah had already arrived.</note>

**VII.** On the assumption of worthy satisfaction, the incarnation was necessary. From the scriptures and the Church Fathers, we deduce that Christ would not have come if sin had been removed: notwithstanding which, Christ as Redeemer was predestined before all creatures.

<meta>Catchword at bottom right: VIII.</meta>

<summary>This page announces the public doctoral defense of Ambrosius Jancko, a Premonstratensian monk, at the University of Vienna in 1758. It lists his academic credentials and presents seven "assertions" or theological propositions regarding the nature of God, the Trinity, and the Incarnation that he intended to defend.</summary>

<keywords>University of Vienna, Ambrosius Jancko, Jesuit education, Nicolaus Muszka, Joseph Redelhamer, Premonstratensians, Scholastic theology, Suarezianism, Trinity, Incarnation, Free Will</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of theological assertions (theses) begun on the previous page, moving from the nature of God to Christology, the mechanics of divine grace, and the authority of the Church. The catchword "XVIII" connects the bottom of the left column to the top of the right.</meta>

**VIII.** We assert that only one person is to be admitted in Christ, and the Blessed Virgin is truly to be called the Mother of God <note>original: "DEIparam." Also known as the *Theotokos*, this title affirms that Mary gave birth to the person of the Word who had assumed human nature.</note>; and that there are two natures in Christ after their union, remaining unmixed.

**IX.** The Word assumed a soul and a body, the latter being subject to suffering by its natural condition; Christ, because of the hypostatic union <note>The union of divine and human natures in the one person of Jesus Christ.</note>, was entirely incapable of sinning.

**X.** We defend the communication of properties <note>original: "communicationem Idiomatum." This is the theological principle where attributes of both the human and divine natures are attributed to the one person of Jesus (e.g., "God died on the cross").</note> in Christ, yet not in the way that the Ubiquitarians <note>A term for certain Lutherans who believed Christ's human body was omnipresent (everywhere) like his divinity.</note> admit.

**XI.** We reject the error of Felix and Elipandus <note>Leaders of the 8th-century Adoptionist heresy in Spain.</note>, who taught that Christ, insofar as he is man, should be called an adopted son.

**XII.** It is certain that there was human knowledge in Christ, and that it was full of all things. Two wills must be admitted in him <note>Against the Monothelite heresy, which claimed Christ had only a divine will.</note>. Holy Father Pope Honorius I was free from Monothelitism in his letters. <note>A defense of the Pope against historical charges that he supported the Monothelite heresy.</note>

**XIII.** Christ was most free in all his works, even in undergoing death: this freedom can be aptly reconciled with his inability to sin, assuming a purely directive precept.

**XIV.** Against the Socinians <note>A group that denied the divinity of Christ and the substitutionary nature of his death.</note>, it is certain that Christ properly made satisfaction for sins: this satisfaction was of simply infinite value. He earned all gifts of grace and glory for both humans and Angels.

**XV.** Christ’s redemption pertains to all human beings, but not likewise to the Demons.

**XVI.** Christ was a Priest in the true sense of the name according to the order of Melchizedek, in whose offering the sacrifice of the Most Holy Eucharist was prefigured.

**XVII.** The necessity of interior prevenient grace <note>Grace that comes before a human act to incline the will toward good.</note> for any salvific work, even for the beginning of faith, is certain against the Pelagians and Semi-Pelagians. Likewise, it is a matter of faith against Jansenius and Quesnel <note>Cornelius Jansen and Pasquier Quesnel, whose views on grace and predestination were condemned by the Church.</note> that in the state of fallen nature, simply sufficient aids of grace are given.

<column-break/>

**XVIII.** The power and efficacy of grace does not consist in a "relatively victorious delight" in the sense of Jansenius; nor in "physical predetermination," or the inspiration of holy love, or "victorious delight" in the sense of certain Catholic [theologians]. Instead, it is best placed in supernatural aid bestowed under the light of conditional knowledge. <note>original: "scientiæ conditionalis." This refers to *Scientia Media* (Middle Knowledge), a Jesuit theological concept developed by Luis de Molina to reconcile human free will with divine grace.</note>

**XIX.** Provision has been made for all regarding the means necessary for salvation.

**XX.** Habitual grace <note>A stable, supernatural disposition that perfects the soul.</note> is a spiritual gift granting the righteous person a right to heavenly glory, and is more probably distinct from the habit of charity. Habitual grace is required for "condign merit" <note>Merit that God is bound to reward because of his promise and the dignity of the grace within the soul.</note>, but it is not likewise required on the part of the work that it be an act elicited or at least commanded by charity.

**XXI.** We gather from the Scriptures and Councils that there are three Theological Virtues: Faith, Hope, and Charity. The assent of faith must be made on account of the testimony of GOD, who is supremely truthful and wise. That Benedict XIV is the true Vicar of Christ can be believed by divine faith. <note>Benedict XIV was the reigning Pope from 1740 to 1758.</note>

**XXII.** The Popes, as true successors of Peter, are superior to Ecumenical Councils, and their judgment is infallible even before the acceptance of the Church. <note>A strong assertion of Papal Infallibility and authority, common in Jesuit theology of this era.</note>

**XXIII.** At all times, explicit faith in the existence of GOD and that He is a Rewarder has been necessary as a means of salvation. Since the promulgation of the Gospel, faith in the Incarnation and the Most Holy Trinity is required by the same necessity.

**XXIV.** Although on its own part hope is most firm, on our part it is not so certain as to exclude all fear.

**XXV.** The motive of charity is the absolute goodness of GOD shining forth in every divine perfection; against the Baians <note>Followers of Michael Baius, who held restrictive views on human nature and grace.</note> it is shown that a love of GOD above all things is possible even as a purely natural act.

<summary>This page lists a series of theological "assertions" or theses covering Christology, the mechanics of divine grace (opposing Jansenism), the nature of the theological virtues, and the supremacy and infallibility of the Pope. It reflects the mid-18th-century Jesuit commitment to Molinism and Ultramontanism in the context of a doctoral defense at the University of Vienna.</summary>

<keywords>Christology, Hypostatic Union, Communication of Idioms, Pelagianism, Jansenism, Molinism, Middle Knowledge, Papal Infallibility, Benedict XIV, Theological Virtues, Merit, Redemption</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on Christology and the nature of the Word into the practical and moral applications of theology: human agency, the nature of law, the classification of sins, and the theology of the sacraments. It continues the numbered list of academic theses. The catchword "X." at the bottom of the left page signals the start of the tenth thesis on the next column, and the catchword "bus" at the bottom of the right page indicates the next word in the phrase "sub speciebus" (under the appearances).</meta>

# ON HUMAN ACTS, LAWS, SINS, AND SACRAMENTS.

->I.<-

A human act is an act performed by a person voluntarily and freely, with attention given to the rule of morality; for it to be imputed as merit or demerit, it must be free with the "freedom of indifference" <note>original: "libertate indifferentiæ." This refers to the capacity of the will to choose between different options or to choose not to act at all.</note>.

II. This freedom is not hindered by remote principles connected to one extreme, provided they are not determined and necessarily required for the act, and can be impeded by that power under which the will operates in an absolute state.

III. If two opinions that are certainly probable arise concerning the morality of an act, there is regularly no obligation to follow the more probable and safer one <note>This refers to the theological system of "Probabilism," which suggests that if an opinion favoring liberty is solid and probable, one is not forced to follow a stricter "safer" law.</note>.

IV. Happiness <note>original: "Beatitudo."</note>, which is the end goal of human acts, is obtained through the intuitive vision of the one and triune GOD, and the love and joy joined with this.

V. The first rule of morals is the eternal law of GOD; from this, all others derive their origin. The natural law is the necessary will of GOD himself, promulgated through the dictates of reason; it does not allow for any dispensation <note>A "dispensation" is an exemption from a law in a particular case.</note>.

VI. The Mosaic law <note>The Old Testament law given to Moses.</note> has GOD as its author; it was subsequently abolished through Christ. However, even as its legal obligation ended, the use of its legal ceremonies did not immediately become illicit.

VII. The Evangelical law <note>The New Testament or Gospel law.</note> first began to be obligatory from Pentecost; it is not to be abolished while the world lasts.

VIII. In the Gospel precepts that are purely positive <note>Laws established by specific decree rather than being inherent in nature.</note>, GOD can grant a dispensation.

IX. For a human law, whether civil or Ecclesiastical, the consent of the people is not required.

<column-break/>

X. Sin is a free transgression of divine law; faith teaches that GOD is not its author. Furthermore, even that which is called the "material" part of sin <note>The physical act itself, as opposed to the "formal" part, which is the rebellious intent.</note> cannot be predetermined by GOD.

XI. Besides mortal sin, there is also venial sin; these do not differ merely by their outcome. The malice of mortal sin is not simply infinite.

XII. Original sin was passed down from Adam to all his descendants, with the exception of the Blessed Virgin, who was immune even from the immediate debt of incurring it <note>A reference to the Immaculate Conception.</note>.

XIII. It is certain by faith that the punishments of the damned will last eternally. Infants who die with original sin <note>Those who die unbaptized.</note> do not undergo the torture of sensible fire <note>This refers to the traditional theological concept of Limbo, where there is a loss of the vision of God but no physical torment.</note>.

XIV. Christ himself is the author of the sacraments of the New Law; for their completion, a merely external intention—in the sense of Catharinus <note>Ambrosius Catharinus (1484–1553) argued that a minister only needed to perform the external rites correctly for a sacrament to be valid, even without internal intent. This text argues that this is not sufficient.</note>—is not enough.

XV. However, the faith of the Minister is not necessary; nor does his wickedness hinder the validity of the Sacrament.

XVI. The grace conferred in Baptism remits all guilt; yet it does not cause this "by the work performed" <note>original: "ex opere operato." A technical term meaning the sacrament works by its own power, provided the recipient places no obstacle.</note> if it is received with an obstacle <note>original: "obice." A spiritual barrier, like lack of contrition.</note>, even if this obstacle is later removed.

XVII. The "matter" <note>The physical substance used, such as oil.</note> of Confirmation was instituted by Christ disjunctively <note>Meaning Christ may have allowed a choice between specific substances.</note>; its Ordinary Minister is the Bishop.

XVIII. The Holy Eucharist was instituted by Christ at the Last Supper, after the legal Paschal lamb, at the same time as the Jews ate it on the day before his death.

XIX. Its matter, besides wine from the vine, is wheaten bread, whether unleavened or leavened; its form, however, is these or similar words: *This is my Body, this is my Blood.*

XX. It is not only a Sacrament, but also a sacrifice. In this, the thing offered is Christ alone, as he is under the appearance— <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "bus" indicates the next word is "species" (appearances).</note>

<summary>This page outlines theological theses regarding human moral agency, the definitions of divine and natural law, the nature of sin (original, mortal, and venial), and the foundational requirements for the Sacraments, particularly Baptism, Confirmation, and the Eucharist.</summary>
<keywords>human acts, free will, eternal law, natural law, original sin, Limbo, Sacraments, Baptism, Eucharist, transubstantiation, intention, merit</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a significant shift in the manuscript. While the previous page concluded a series of theological theses regarding human acts and sacraments, this page serves as a formal title and dedication for a specific dissertation by Joseph von Sonnenfels. It transitions from general scholastic theology to a specialized study of Hebrew and Rabbinic law.</meta>

<sig>a 2</sig>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring floral motifs, scrolls, and a central basket or vessel overflowing with fruit and flowers.</image-desc>

# A DISSERTATION
## BY JOSEPH VON SONNENFELS
### THE SON,
### ON COMBINING THE STUDY
### OF THE RABBIS AND RITUALS
### WITH HEBREW.
<note>Joseph von Sonnenfels (1733–1817) was a prominent Austrian jurist and advisor to Empress Maria Theresa. Here, he is identified as "the son" to distinguish him from his father, Alois von Sonnenfels, a scholar of Oriental languages.</note>

-> - - *Thus one thing demands the aid of the other* <note>original: "Alterius sic altera poscit opem res." A quote from the Roman poet Horace's *Ars Poetica*, suggesting that different fields of study require mutual support.</note> : **HORACE.** <-

With the greatness of YOUR MAJESTY preserved, MOST AUGUST EMPRESS, I pray that you might descend even to our humble concerns; and from there, take for YOURSELF that glory which is always inherent in YOUR works. You possess this unique distinction: that by the same arts with which you long ago made the minds of all people your own, you make whatever honor of ours your own as well. Sustain, therefore—since we, provoked by YOUR CLEMENCY, are unable to restrain ourselves—sustain (I say) these prayers breaking forth as witnesses of our gratitude: may you complete a lifespan as long as you deserve! For you will never fail to preserve that spirit of yours.

<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

<summary>This is the title page and formal dedication of a dissertation by Joseph von Sonnenfels the younger, focusing on the integration of Rabbinic traditions and Hebrew studies. The text is dedicated to the Empress (likely Maria Theresa) in a highly stylized, laudatory Latin prose.</summary>

<keywords>Joseph von Sonnenfels, Hebrew studies, Rabbinic law, Horace, Maria Theresa, academic dissertation, 18th century, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Joseph von Sonnenfels’ dissertation to the Empress (Maria Theresa). The previous page established the author’s humble submission and gratitude; this page expands on the specific academic goal of the work: the revival of Hebrew and Rabbinic studies under her patronage.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographic ornament consisting of a central floral motif flanked by stars and scrolling flourishes.</image-desc>

Then indeed—and my prophecy does not fail me—through YOUR MOST AUGUST MUNIFICENCE, the sciences which are now budding will, once they have matured, transfer YOUR memory to immortality. Although you have long since anticipated this immortality through your great deeds, it would be more reverent to keep your deeds whole and untouched in our thoughts rather than touching upon them briefly and in fragments, as we are less able to celebrate them as befits YOUR high station. For it usually follows that those things about which one remains silent appear as great as they truly are. <margin>(1) A line from the Panegyric to Trajan. <note>A speech by Pliny the Younger praising the Emperor Trajan, used here to compare the Empress to the Roman ideal.</note></margin> Indeed, it is no wonder there is a present silence, into which YOUR GREATNESS compels us. We, who are not sufficient for ourselves, would give thanks in vain; but posterity, to whom YOUR LIBERALITY <note>original: *Liberalitas*</note> is passed down undiminished, even to future generations, will always be indebted. Unless, however, it pleases you to be mentioned: because while you undertook the whole of the state, you directed your care to individual parts as if for their sake alone; and we wonder that nothing at all was left out, nothing unconsidered. Among innumerable other things, we also owe this to you: that we see the Hebrew and other languages joyfully blooming again; by which very thing you have driven away from us the accusation of ignorance, which we often (and not undeservedly) endured. Increase, LADY, your gift! Indeed, complete it! For what is missing for the perfection of Hebrew literature is the study of rituals and the Rabbis; but so

---
<margin>(1) A line from the Panegyric to Trajan.</margin>

<column-break/>

<sig>a 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: que</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographic ornament consisting of a central floral motif flanked by stars and scrolling flourishes.</image-desc>

so missing, that the former without the latter are like a trunk—a shapeless work—and of no use except for the name. This is a type of expenditure worthy of so great a PRINCESS and one certainly deserving of immortality: a work of YOUR height alone, to bless your subjects in every part. And indeed, the CLEMENCY OF THE LADY <note>original: *Clementia Dominae*</note> bids me be confident that this will certainly happen; for once incited, liberality—the beauty of which usage itself commends—does not know how to stop. <margin>(1) Pliny, *Letters*, Book V, 12.</margin> Let us only act so that she may consider her investment to be growing through our use of it. Therefore, until we are granted our prayers, anticipate in your minds the greatness of the expected benefit.

I would not wish, Kind Readers, <note>The text uses "L. L.", an abbreviation for *Lectoribus Benevolis*, a common address to the "Benevolent Readers" in early modern books.</note> for you to think you owe me anything; nor will I insolently claim for myself the role of teacher. I have cursed those, and always will curse them, who seek a position of authority in letters at a time when, due to their lack of skill, their reasoning cannot yet be taken into account. They are often much like the little Greek Phormio <margin>(2) Cicero, *On the Orator*, Book I.</margin> <note>Phormio was a philosopher who notoriously gave a lecture on the duties of a general to Hannibal, one of history's greatest military commanders, who found the lecture ridiculous.</note> disputing about the method of war in the presence of Hannibal. Indeed, I am deterred by the warning of Seneca <margin>(3) *Letters to Lucilius*, Letter 5.</margin> lest I act in the manner of those who *desire not to progress, but to be noticed*; and I shudder at the outcomes denounced by the same man for such daring: *lest those very things, through which we wish to gain admiration, be ridiculous and hateful*. I point out, therefore, him from whom it is not unbecoming for you to be taught, and by whom

---
(1) Pliny, *Letters*, Book V, 12. (2) Cicero, *On the Orator*, Book I. (3) *Letters to Lucilius*, Letter 5.

<vocab>Munificence (Generosity), Liberality (Giving), Clemency (Mercy/Grace), Hebrew literature, Study of the Rabbis, Phormio, Seneca, Pliny</vocab>

He humbly positions himself not as a master, but as a student avoiding the vanity of "pseudo-intellectuals" like the legendary Phormio.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface of Joseph von Sonnenfels’ work. While the previous page addressed the Empress and the revival of Hebrew studies, this section pivots to a personal acknowledgment of the author’s father, Lippmann (Perlinus) Sonnenfels, and the noble patrons who supported Joseph’s education.</meta>

<image-desc>At the top of both pages, there is a decorative typographic ornament consisting of scrolls and stars.</image-desc>

...with him as the author, I consider it most honorable for me to attempt something first. My excellent father <note>original: *Genitor meus optimus*. This refers to Perlinus (Lippmann) Sonnenfels, a Jewish convert to Christianity and professor of Oriental languages, who was the true scholar behind much of this research.</note>, whom I name with a preface of honor, just as in his other scholarly labors, did not disdain to consider my own efforts of some value in this work as well. While he handed over his reflections to me to be polished by my pen <note>original: *stylo perpoliendas*</note>—such is his indulgence toward me (perhaps because he desired my mind to be raised to further things by such encouragement)—he not only permitted but commanded me to prefix my name to this as a preface. Nevertheless, he acknowledges this "offspring" as his own no less for that reason, having entrusted the ownership to me alone in confidence. Hence, it is not at all the glory of publishing my own wit (which, if nature gave me any talent, remains uncultivated) that brings me forward, but obedience, and the endeavor

-> to fulfill the mind <-
-> of a revered Father. <-

It is right for the Reader to judge that whatever I shall say, he says it to you. He, since he took upon himself the trouble of educating me, provided not only the columns <note>The main structural arguments of the text.</note> but even the smallest supports; nothing is mine except a tenacious memory of his teaching, a reasoning acquired through constant diligence, the organization, and the style. If in that "nothing" I do not displease you, O Learned Readers <note>original: *L. L.*, short for *Lectores Benevoli*, a standard greeting to "kind" or "learned" readers.</note>, you have made me blessed!

And would that I might so fulfill the hope he conceived of me, as He, in his duty as a skillful and pious father, failed in nothing that could be fulfilled by me. Grant, I beseech you, O Learned Readers, to my gratitude, while

<meta>catchword: it</meta>

<column-break/>

eager to witness it openly, I seize this occasion to ask that you waive something of your rights and the water-clocks! <note>original: *clepsydris*. In ancient courts, water-clocks were used to limit the time of a speaker; the author is asking the readers for their patience and extra time.</note> For to this Father, common to all children, I perhaps owe not only my life and the means of life, but that most ample thing: faith. I owe it to His care: that by the **MOST SERENE HOUSE OF DIETRICHSTEIN** <note>A powerful Austrian noble family who acted as patrons to the Sonnenfels family.</note>, I was not only raised and educated (a munificence in which this truly **PRINCELY HOUSE** is almost prodigal toward countless others) but so educated that, while the Very Illustrious Lord C. von Holbein <note>likely Baron Carl von Holbein</note>—whose memory I shall eternally honor in the place of a second father for the extraordinary favors he provided—took it upon himself to mold my tender years, more pliable for the shaping of the mind, **SHE** <note>The House of Dietrichstein, personified.</note> most kindly saw to it that I was imbued with letters; nor does she disdain to cherish me today with **HER POWERFUL PROTECTION**; may she never disdain it! So I pray: if, as is the only thing I can do, I shall always present a spirit not degenerate to my **MOST SERENE NURTURERS**, and as long as I live, I shall profess these immense benefits with the deepest devotion. Therefore, this great Patronage, and that love of letters, and whatever non-ignorance of them there is (if there be any), are the fruits of his anxiety. Now finally, this very fact, that I am able to testify gratitude for such great merits in me, I owe to this sweetest Father: he has so anticipated everything for me by his benefits!

When my soul has performed its duty of piety, I return to you, O Learned Readers, and I suppose that for me, with you...

<meta>This page continues the preface where Joseph von Sonnenfels explains the purpose of his Hebrew studies. While the previous page established that the work was largely based on the teachings of his father, Perlinus Sonnenfels, this section defends the study of the Hebrew language not as an end in itself, but as a vital "key" to understanding ancient laws and culture. He argues against those who prioritize only secular or imperial studies ("Caesar") at the expense of sacred antiquity.</meta>

<image-desc>Decorative typographic ornament at the top of both pages consisting of floral scrolls and three stars.</image-desc>

...from this it follows: the knowledge of Hebrew letters is useful only insofar as it prepares the student and does not delay them. Those may disagree, for whom

-> *the plan of studies lies in CAESAR alone.* <-
<note>By "Caesar," the author likely refers to a purely secular or legal education focused on the Holy Roman Empire's laws and administration, contrasting it with the "sacred" study of Hebrew.</note>

as far as I am concerned, they may; for my part, my opinion has always stood firm: that these letters are a stimulus for learning, not the source of satisfaction. For what is being sought? Truly, whatever is sought, you will lack it if you choose to get stuck here. To be sure, every thing is acquired either for the sake of decorum, or for utility alone, or for necessity: and I say confidently that none of these can be obtained through the Hebrew language alone. Certainly, it is a beautiful thing to know the essence of everything, simply so that it may be known; indeed, I believe anyone is so persuaded of this,

-> *who is able to avoid a cliff and an open well,* <-
<note>A proverbial way of saying "anyone with common sense."</note>

that I do not fear this little book will ever fall into the hands of one who thinks otherwise. If, however, it does fall there, I even now divine its fate: it will displease; it will be cast aside; it will even be scorned. Would that we might owe a debt of thanks to our fate if it is scorned by such as those—provided it wins the approval of the better sort, and carries the votes of those who agree with me that all knowledge is both beautiful and pleasant! Therefore, Hebrew study is beautiful for its own sake: if, however, it can be called "study" to hold only the first strokes and the bare meanings of words, or even grammatical inflections; and to have mastered—as one who has extended their boundaries most widely—certain idioms and <term>phraseology</term> <note>original Greek: Φρασεολογίαν. This refers to the specific way phrases are constructed in a language.</note>, so that

<column-break/>

one has it clearly perceived or familiar; surely such a person possesses the language, but the language only. Just as it is the first step toward the understanding of the Holy Scriptures <note>original: S. S. Codicum (Sacrorum Codices)</note>, so it is not yet the understanding itself: no more, indeed, than he who holds the key to unlock a treasure, but is never going to unlock it, is rich. In this empty and, so to speak, material possession of a language, if any beauty exists, it is surely meager; but it would yield a great deal of profit if the study of the Rabbis and their rituals were added to it. For in that way, we lay open the hiding places and recesses of things deeply (or at least mostly) unknown, as if they were another world; and it will then be easy to enter the sanctuaries of venerable antiquity. These are not those trifles, or things like them, such as:

> *The nurse of Anchises, the name and country of Archemorus’ stepmother—how many years Acestes lived? How many jars of wine the Sicilian gave to the Phrygians?*
<note>These are quotes from the Roman satirist Juvenal (Satire VII), mocking scholars who obsess over trivial, useless mythological details.</note>

In such matters, because we consider them fine, we take diligent care that our boys do not stumble; but we will not, I say, be learning those things. Instead, we learn what might move us more: the nature of the laws brought by the wisest Lawgiver <note>Original: Nomotheta. This refers to Moses.</note>, the ceremonies, his worship, the morals of the people who were once the clerical tribe, the deeds, the opinions, and the customs of a nation as great as the Jews once were, and are today. No one among us would deny that these things are honorable, we who place so much praise on the antiquities of the Greeks and Romans; nor is it rare...

<summary>Sonnenfels argues that learning the Hebrew language is merely a "key" that is useless unless used to unlock the "treasure" of Jewish history, law, and Rabbinic tradition. He contrasts the profound study of the "Lawgiver" (Moses) with the trivial mythological pedantry often taught in classical schools.</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew studies, linguistics vs. understanding, Moses, Lawgiver, Jewish antiquities, Rabbinic ritual, education philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Hebrew studies. While the previous page argued that Hebrew is a "key" to understanding and not just an end in itself, this page compares the prestige of classical Roman and Greek antiquarianism with the neglected study of Jewish history and scholars. The author argues that Jewish figures and institutions deserve the same intellectual respect as their Roman counterparts.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring floral scrolls and three stars at the top of the left column.</image-desc>

...over a woman’s robe or a *bulla*—the token of a free-born boy—we fight as if for our household gods. If these things are worthy of investigation for the sake of beauty, then how much more worthy is that majestic temple of Divine Majesty, the sacrifices usually offered there, the entire liturgy, and furthermore, those "mother-schools" of famous men: the schools of Babylon, Jerusalem, Jaffa, Lydda, Tiberias, and others. Consider at least the Sanhedrin <note>The supreme council and tribunal of Jews in ancient Israel.</note>, a tribunal renowned for its severity, its justice, and the order of its judgments. For there are those who, through sleepless nights and great expense, have unearthed or restored the records and names of Roman consuls, rescuing from oblivion men like Paullus, Gaius, and Scipio, as well as the legal disagreements of the jurists. Since we do not dispute the name and merit of these historians, let us admit that equal merit belongs to those who bring into the light and brush the dust off such figures as Shammai, Hillel, Zaccheides, Gamaliel, Menahem, Nicodemus, Samuel the Small, James, and many others like them. I also ask for fairness: that it be held in the same value if someone chooses to devote their labor to Hebrew monuments as someone who restores—and not infrequently substitutes—Roman gravestones and sepulchral <term>epigrams [original Greek: ἐπιγράμματα]</term>; I would not even exempt a scholar like Gruter <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627) was a famous Dutch classicist known for his massive collection of Roman inscriptions.</note> from this comparison. I wish for this, since even today there are those who would pay a high price for the clay lamp of Epictetus or the tub of the Cynic <note>A reference to Diogenes of Sinope, who famously lived in a tub or large ceramic jar.</note>, and who go mad for the ancient statues of Damasippus...

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring floral scrolls and three stars at the top of the right column, matching the left.</image-desc>

...I buy your things; there would also be those who would want, if it were ever offered, to redeem for a small price the tiaras of Eliezer or the shield of Joshua. Since public idols and private household gods, great and small, are preserved everywhere like <term>treasure [original Greek: γάζα]</term>, then any surviving insignias of Hebrew antiquity ought to be valued at any price. But it is usually the case that if someone happens to possess a cushion of Jupiter, or perhaps even the "horn of Achelous" <note>A symbol of plenty, similar to the cornucopia.</note>, he thinks himself as rich as Croesus. Yet he would not exchange the ivory staff which the Roman struck against the Gaul to his own ruin <note>Refers to the legend of Marcus Papirius, who struck a Gallic soldier with his staff during the sack of Rome, sparking a massacre.</note> for the rod of Moses—not even, I truly believe, on "Attalic conditions" <note>A Roman proverb for the most extravagant terms or wealth possible, named after the wealthy kings of Pergamum.</note>. These views of less-educated men, who do not lead the way in literary circles, offer no argument for being discouraged, especially when many memorable men from every age and place can be brought forward as evidence to the contrary. Because I do not have time to recount them all, and lest I seem to prefer one over the other, I will not name them individually; I am content to have pointed out a class of men who attained the "broad stripe" <note>The *latus clavus*, a broad purple stripe on the tunic that marked a Roman senator, here used metaphorically for high intellectual or social rank.</note> of the sciences, and attained it precisely because they excelled in Hebrew studies. The common estimation and reverence with which people rise for such men, and the encouragements and efforts of those who know the subject to make the path clear for others, easily prove and defend the dignity of these sciences. If only their incentives were more powerful and their "tickle" of curiosity more sharp! But indeed, how few—

<summary>Sonnenfels argues that the study of Hebrew antiquity, its legal scholars (like Hillel and Shammai), and its institutions (like the Sanhedrin) is just as historically valuable as the study of Roman consuls and Greek philosophers. He critiques the contemporary obsession with classical relics while Jewish history remains "covered in dust."</summary>

<keywords>Sanhedrin, Hillel, Shammai, antiquarianism, Jewish schools, Roman history, academic prestige, Hebrew studies, classical vs sacred history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument for the necessity of studying Hebrew and Rabbinic literature. While the previous page focused on the historical and cultural value of Jewish scholars (like Hillel and Gamaliel), this page shifts to a practical theological defense: using Rabbinic knowledge to protect the integrity of the Latin Bible (the Vulgate) against modern critics.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographic ornament featuring a central cluster of stars flanked by symmetrical floral scrolls at the top of the page.</image-desc>

No one is so patient that they wish to learn what they will not eventually use. (1) **For while a sense of propriety only excites exceptional minds, utility persuades even those of average talent.** The use of this study [of Rabbinic literature] alongside Hebrew letters is multifaceted; without the former, the latter is useless. It manifests itself first—and I consider this the chief point—in that once we have acquired some knowledge of the writings of the Rabbis and their antiquity, we can defend the version of the Bible received by the Church <note>The Vulgate, the Latin translation of the Bible primarily produced by St. Jerome in the 4th century.</note>, which many today attack, less out of confidence in their own knowledge than out of the ignorance of others. It is as the saying goes: <note>original Hebrew: לא מפני אהבת מרדכי אלא מפני שנאת המן</note> *Not for the love of Mordecai, but out of hatred for Haman.* <note>A Jewish idiom meaning that someone performs a seemingly good act not out of devotion to the cause, but out of spite for an enemy.</note> Indeed, the work of Jerome is abundantly protected by both the opinion and the authority of the Church (hence I speak here of its utility, not its absolute necessity). But if—as is the duty of any theologian—one must compare it, whether in a brief skirmish or a direct head-on confrontation, with any of those critics who, unmoved by the Church's defining law, constantly appeal back to the "original" source; then truly the times require such help and such defenders. It is painful to hear from those men who often criticize Saint Jerome, while they themselves (as Hesiod says)
> <note>original Greek: οὐδ᾽ ἴσασιν ὅσῳ πλέον ἥμισυ παντὸς</note>
> *neither know how much more the half is than the whole.* <note>A Greek proverb suggesting that a simple, honest portion is better than a large, ill-gotten or misunderstood one; here used to mock critics who lack the full context of Jerome's work.</note>

Certainly, if they were to be compared with him—if you look at his labor, and what other things he wrote besides,

<margin>(1) Pliny the Younger, *Letters*, Book 8, Letter 14.</margin>

<column-break/>

other things which he dictated, the business he handled, the consultations for friends and even the family matters to which he was available, the battles for the faith he took up and concluded—and all within what a short time; I am stunned. This one phrase seems to sufficiently express the sacred awe that overcomes me when I contemplate these things. The translation of the Scriptures was a work which a whole lifetime would barely suffice for; it is full of the highest accuracy, antiquity, and perpetual harmony. Yet, because he lacked defenders, someone dared to say: (1) *Jerome and Origen <note>An early Christian scholar and theologian (c. 185–253 AD) known for his massive work on the Old Testament.</note> would have handled these studies more successfully if they had been more skilled.* As if he himself knew better! Meanwhile, that man [Jerome] is most <term>divine [Lat. Divinus]</term> precisely where he occasionally turns away from the literal word (for he never completely departs from it). In such places, his version is always defended by either excellent reasoning, antiquity, or authority; and indeed, his work is filled with these qualities. I happened to hear many such examples from my Father during his analysis of the scripture itself; for he always maintains this method: to place Jerome’s version in safety everywhere. Concerning this, the set limits and even more my own weakness forbid me from being more long-winded; I appeal, therefore, to those things which are found throughout this little work on this subject. A convenient passage occurs, however, which places before our eyes the enormous industry of our translator. Isaiah threatens, among other punishments for sins...

<margin>(1) In the preface to Lightfoot. <note>Referring to John Lightfoot (1602–1675), an English churchman and Hebraist.</note></margin>

He expresses "sacred awe" at Jerome's immense scholarly output and criticizes those who claim the Church Father was unskilled.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s demonstration of how Saint Jerome (Hieronymus) used ancient Jewish traditions and Hebrew linguistic nuances to inform his Latin translation of the Bible (the Vulgate). The previous page established that Rabbinic knowledge is a tool for defending the Vulgate; here, the author provides specific examples of biblical measurements and cultural references that Jerome "decoded" using these sources.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>

-> ❦ ❦ ❦ <-

...mentioned the sterility of the earth and says (1) **"For ten acres of vineyard shall yield one bath, and the seed of a homer shall yield an ephah"** <note>Isaiah 5:10. The prophet is describing a harvest so poor it is practically a total loss.</note>: *word for word* <note>original Greek: κατά ῥῆμα</note> it will be so. However, Jerome substituted "a little flask" <note>original Latin: laguncula</note> for **Bath**, and "thirty bushels" <note>original Latin: modios</note> for the **Homer**, and "three bushels" for the **Ephah**. From where does he derive these measures, or what kind are they? Surely from the remnants of the *deuterosis* <note>A term used by Church Fathers to refer to the Jewish Oral Law or Mishnah</note>, which preserved the quantity of these measures for us. I will refrain from transcribing the specific passages here, since Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a famous Hebraist</note> produces them—or at least indicates them—under the entries for **Bath**, **Homer**, and **Ephah**. Meanwhile, it is a marvel that in place of the *Bath* (which is a liquid measure, equal in size to the *Ephah* used for dry goods), Jerome placed "one little flask." Since he maintained the proportion of "three bushels," I had believed "three flasks" should have been placed there. But he did this most keenly and with wonderful proportion; for when an equal sterility is denounced for both fields and vineyards, and the *Homer* (which he elsewhere (2) translates as "thirty bushels") yields only "three bushels" to be expected from the sowing—coming out to one bushel for every ten—he added no more than "one little flask" for ten acres of vineyard to demonstrate that same equality of loss. This is a specimen of his accuracy. Another shall be this example of antiquity (3) **"As the binding of a stone in a heap, so is he who gives honor to a fool"** <note>Proverbs 26:8</note>: as it stands, it is *according to the binding of a stone in a stone-heap* (for so I translate it; for "sling,"

---

(1) Chapter 5, verse 10. (2) Ezekiel chapter 45, verse 11. (3) Proverbs chapter 26, verse 8.

<column-break/>

-> ❦ ❦ ❦ <-

"sling" or "catapult" does not please me, which are the meanings Buxtorf places under **margemah**, for which he is to be blamed shortly hereafter) *so is he who gives honor to a fool*. Our Saint [Jerome] translates: *As he who casts a stone into the heap of Mercury, etc.* But from where does that come, and what is this "heap of Mercury"? It is taken again from the Hebrews, from whom light is shed on the matter: (1) **"Rabbi Ishmael says: the worship of Marculis (Mercury) consists of three stones, one beside the other, etc."** <note>Reference to a passage in the Talmud, Tractate Avoda Zarah, describing pagan rituals.</note> Since passersby saw these erected stones, they would throw a stone on top out of respect, until it rose into a heap. Now Jerome has interpreted this correctly, and better than Buxtorf did in his smaller lexicon; Buxtorf did not grasp this interpretation, even though in his Rabbinic Lexicon he cites—and if I am not mistaken, credits—this passage under the word **Marculis**. Let these serve as a sort of first taste and evidence. 

Jerome deferred no less to authority than to reason and antiquity. In many places where he found the original text to be ambiguous, he followed the Chaldean Paraphrasts <note>The *Targums*, ancient Aramaic translations and interpretations of the Hebrew Bible</note>; thus, the Chaldean language <note>Aramaic</note> must also be called upon. And called upon it must be! For let no one who knows only those few small parts of the Book of Daniel imagine that he is a master of Chaldean. The language is altogether more vast, and besides other books, it is celebrated in the **Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah)</note> by the author Rabbi Simeon Ben Jochai, and in the *Great Assembly* <note>original Aramaic: Idra Rabba</note> and the *Lesser Assembly* <note>original Aramaic: Idra Suta</note>.

---

(1) Tractate Avoda Sarah [Zarah], page 49.

<summary>The author explains how Saint Jerome used Jewish oral traditions and Aramaic translations to accurately translate obscure biblical measurements and cultural references, such as the "heap of Mercury" in Proverbs. He argues that a true theologian must master not just Hebrew, but also Aramaic (Chaldean) and Rabbinic literature to fully understand the Bible.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that Christian scholars must study Jewish sources to defend the Vulgate (the Latin Bible). The previous page ended while discussing the authority of Saint Jerome’s translation; this page lists specific Rabbinic texts and the "Aramaic Paraphrases" (Targums) as essential tools for understanding the biblical text and Church liturgy.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>

-> ❦ ❦ ❦ <-

The tractate **Sotah** <note>A section of the Mishnah concerning the ritual for a woman suspected of adultery.</note> (with the Father as judge) authored by **Akiba**; the **Jerusalem Talmud**, whose author was Rabbi Jochanan; and especially the **Paraphrases of Scripture** <note>Known as the Targums—Aramaic translations and expansions of the Hebrew Bible.</note> are held in the highest value and honor. The convert **Onkelos** <note>A famous proselyte to Judaism and author of the standard Aramaic translation of the Torah.</note> provided the version of the Law; he is said to have seen to it that Gamaliel was carried out with the most expensive funeral honors (1). **Jonathan** translated the Prophets, although a version of the Law by him also exists, but one which the Talmud does not recognize in the tractate *Megillah*. The third Paraphrast is the **Jerusalem** one, whom Augustine Calmet <note>A French Benedictine monk (1672–1757) famous for his Bible dictionary and commentaries.</note> frequently praises. These versions are venerable to Jews and Christians alike, both for their antiquity and their faithfulness. They were regarded as the native version for the Jews during the time when they were torn away from one another and dragged here and there into captivity, losing their knowledge of the noble Hebrew language; in its place, they drank in the Aramaic <note>original: Chaldæam. In this period, Aramaic was often called Chaldean.</note> of the dominant nation. The reliability of these versions shines forth especially in those places which prophesy concerning the **MESSIAH**, where they frequently and clearly use the term **King MESSIAH** <note>original Aramaic: מלכא משיחא (Malka Meshicha)</note>. Such passages can be used with great benefit against a denying Jew; when he tries to twist the meaning of a verse toward something else or attempts to elude it, he must be pinned down by the authority of this text, which he is bound to revere. If the **critics** <note>original Greek: φιλοψόγοι (philopsogoi), meaning those who love to find fault.</note> found more people equipped with these resources, how much more sparingly would they hurl their attacks! Certainly, as it would be easy to assert by hand both the authority

---

<margin>(1) Tractate Avoda Sarah, page 11, and the Additions (Tosafot) to Shabbat, chapter 7.</margin>

<column-break/>

-> ❦ ❦ ❦ <-

...of the Church and the knowledge of the man <note>Referring to Saint Jerome.</note>; at the same time, it would put the mockers to shame. Indeed, one could even charge against them, press them, and demonstrate: the fault is not in the translation, but in themselves, who are ignorant of these things which they criticize. The use of both fields of study <note>Hebrew language and Rabbinic tradition.</note> is of no less value, especially the knowledge of ancient rituals, for illustrating the **liturgy of the Church**, which has undoubtedly adopted many—truly, very many—things from them. And someone rightly said that **Christianity is nothing but true Judaism**. I will never humble myself so far as to yield the name of "Israelite" to the Jew. Therefore, a certain affinity exists between us and the **Ancient Synagogue**, which is discerned even today in many ceremonies of the Church; it will be discerned, I say, if those ceremonies are not ignored, and if we understand the reason for receiving this one or boasting of that one. Truly, the harvest of this matter is rich, but few have put their sickle into it. **Spencer** <note>John Spencer (1630–1693), an English theologian whose work "On the Ritual Laws of the Hebrews" was a foundational text in comparative religion.</note> has some material on the legitimate rituals of the Hebrews, but only a little, and he stops almost entirely within the limits of the Bible. You might find things in other authors, mentioned as if by chance, but they are rarer, and to collect them from such a vastness of books would extend beyond a human lifetime. Hence, it is more profitable to drink from the sources themselves, with both less labor and more purity. And so, there are many things that meet our eyes daily; and by the very fact that they are so common, they are less noticed, even though their explanation is hidden deep in **Hebrew antiquity**, and cannot be found from any other source.

<sig>b</sig>

<summary>The author argues that Aramaic Targums and Talmudic texts are essential for Christian scholars to defend the Messiah's prophecies and understand the Jewish roots of Church liturgy. He asserts that Christianity is the "true Judaism" and encourages studying primary Hebrew sources over secondary academic summaries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that a deep understanding of Jewish ritual and rabbinic literature provides essential context for Christian practices and scholarly defense. The previous page ended while discussing how ancient versions of Scripture—like the Targums—contain prophecies that point toward Christ. This page transitions into comparing specific Catholic liturgical rites, like the Holy Saturday ceremonies, to Jewish precedents.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>b 2</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring floral scrolls and three stars.</image-desc>

than it is sought. The burning of the <term>Chrism</term> <note>Consecrated oil used in Catholic sacraments like Baptism and Confirmation.</note> and such things on the <term>Sabbath before Easter</term> <note>Holy Saturday.</note> is well known; why is it burned? Why in the vestibule of the Church, but outside of it? Why on the <term>Preparation for Easter</term> <note>original: "parasceve paschae." Often referring to Good Friday, here used in the context of the vigil rituals.</note>? Or am I mistaken if I seek a similarity to the <term>night of the removal of leaven</term> <note>original: "ליל בעור חמץ" (leil bi'ur chametz). The Jewish ritual of searching for and burning leavened bread before Passover.</note>? I heard many things of this kind from my Father, and as many as ever had him as a professor will testify that they heard them too: especially what he discussed concerning the <term>rites of the Mass</term>, which would bring a blush to those stubborn people who proclaim that everything among the Catholics is handled rashly or according to whim.

Now, to this twofold use must be added the honor which this kind of knowledge (neglected by us until now, and for that reason made to seem greater by those who seize the first available opportunity to insult us) would win for the clergy, to whom these things especially belong: namely, as much honor as the ignorance of these things now brings them contempt. Conversely, that stupor will disappear, in which we have been held as if fascinated by some who have ruled over such matters as if by tyranny, and who have possessed our agreement through our own sluggishness. How many will we be able to charge with manifest theft, if we become more familiar with antiquity? For it is an old habit to steal from others to seek honor for oneself, and it has passed into a proverb among the Rabbis: <term>The wine belongs to the master, but thanks are given to the cupbearer</term> <note>original: "חמרא למריה וטבותא לשקיא" (Chamra le-marei ve-tavuta le-shakya). A Talmudic proverb meaning the servant gets the credit for the master's gift.</note>. But if anyone enjoys, along with <term>Flaccus</term> <note>The Roman poet Horace.</note>:

> — — Bringing things unmentioned
> *To be read by talents and eyes, and held in hands:*

he too will be able to pluck and sell it as his own; that is the simplest method of publishing commentaries. And if it is true, as <term>Secundus</term> <note>Pliny the Younger.</note> says (1), that size adds a certain authority and beauty to the volumes themselves, then this is the way to publish the most beautiful commentaries; for a most ready abundance surpasses those from whom no one has compiled anything. Therefore, he who does not have a <term>heart of horn</term> <note>original: "cornea fibra." A classical idiom for a dull or unfeeling mind.</note> would prefer that others marvel at him, rather than he at others. 

For those who act and will act sincerely, their reputation remains and will remain. Nor is it an inglorious labor to sprinkle things gathered from elsewhere to illustrate a point, provided it is done by citing the author. For the diligence with which those who collect for the common good serves the community, and they earn a common merit without envy. Thus <term>John Lightfoot</term> <note>A 17th-century English Hebraist known for using the Talmud to explain the New Testament.</note> is read, used, and appreciated, and most recently <term>Augustin Calmet</term> <note>A French Benedictine scholar (1672–1757) who wrote extensive biblical commentaries.</note> heard his own praises while still living. Although both contain little in the way of original arguments, they nevertheless have a most well-stocked storehouse for them. They illuminate with their excerpts and (what I consider most important), since they have done these things which would have had to be done by others if they had not provided them, they have prepared a much more efficient path to greater things; and perhaps they have even added a spur, so that—

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the necessity of Hebrew studies for the clergy. Following the previous page's mention of how ignorance of these matters can be a source of shame, the author now asserts that mastering Hebrew is not merely useful but a mandatory requirement for serious theology, as decreed by the highest authorities.</meta>

...so that it may also please others to run toward the same goal. If these pursuits promise utility, I have also asserted with the best of reasons the necessity of joining these studies: in this, I shall rely on the opinion of the **Most August Decree** <note>Likely referring to a specific imperial or papal mandate promoting Oriental languages in universities.</note>, which introduced Hebrew among other subjects in such a way that it commanded it to be learned by any theologian no less than theology itself. Therefore, regarding that which has already been decreed, it is no longer permissible to question; rather, it is established that Hebrew is entirely necessary.

This necessity is divided into different branches, depending on the various ends for which Hebrew was introduced. These ends are: both so that the **Sacred Codices** <note>The original manuscripts and texts of the Bible.</note> may be understood more clearly, and so that the "blindness which has happened to Israel," as St. Paul says <note>original: "D. Paulus." Referring to Romans 11:25.</note>, may be dispelled. I believe these matters are truly beyond dispute, just as this is: that the means which contribute most to achieving an end are necessary—not by a harsh and inevitable necessity (the only kind recognized by those who will do nothing unless forced, and whom no eloquence can persuade), but by that moral necessity which anyone "formed of better clay" <note>original: "meliore luto effictus." A classical idiom meaning someone of refined character or superior intellect.</note> considers a law.

Indeed, whether for the Old or the New Law, these studies are effective. For the Old: because there have been no lack of distinguished commentators among the **Rabbis**, comparing word for word, ruminating, explaining, and harmonizing wherever a conflict seems to appear, being accurate and sharp. It is for this reason that to the edi-

<column-break/>

<sig>b 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: scere</meta>

-tion of the Bible, the following interpreters have been added: **Radak** <note>original: "רדק" (R-D-K). An acronym for Rabbi David Kimchi (c. 1160–1235).</note>, as he is called by a name formed from only the initial letters, *Rabbi David Kimchi*, who explains the grammatical analysis and the literal sense; as do *Aben Esra* <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167).</note> and **Jarchi** <note>Rashi (Solomon ben Isaac, 1040–1105), the most famous medieval commentator. "Jarchi" was a common but mistaken name for him in early modern Christian scholarship.</note>, who handles the Talmudic interpretations; and *Baal Hathurim* <note>Jacob ben Asher (c. 1269–1343), known for his mystical and numerical/gematria commentaries.</note>, who treats mystical expositions. There are also fragments of *Saadia Gaon* <note>The 10th-century Babylonian scholar and philosopher.</note>, as well as *Rabbi Levi Ben Gerschon* <note>Also known as Gersonides (1288–1344).</note> on certain books. Furthermore, the **Chaldean Paraphrase** <note>The Targum, an ancient Aramaic translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> is added and placed alongside them so that where these commentators are less faithful in controversial passages, they may be corrected.

The **Midrashim** <note>Rabbinic homiletical commentaries that often use stories and parables.</note> are interpretations of the **Pentateuch** <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note> (the Psalms being collected into a separate volume), and the "five smaller books" as they are called: Ruth, the Song of Songs, Ecclesiastes, Lamentations, and the Book of Esther. These are called **Midrash Rabba** <note>original: "מדרש רבה" (The Great Midrash).</note> and were produced by the same authors to whom we owe the **Mishnah** <note>The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions, forming the core of the Talmud.</note>. 

It must be admitted that many trivial little stories are contained therein, but also many excellent things that illuminate the text. **Wagenseil** <note>Johann Christoph Wagenseil (1633–1705), a prominent Christian Hebraist known for studying Jewish texts to engage in interreligious debate.</note> appropriately declares his opinion on the use of such things in the preface to his *Fiery Darts* <note>original: "Tela Ignea Satanae," a famous collection of Jewish anti-Christian polemics accompanied by his own refutations.</note>: "*It serves especially*" (argues the man, who is great in these matters) "*to illuminate the Holy Scriptures; and it contributes so much to our proper understanding of the words, laws, customs, and institutions of the ancient Jews mentioned therein, that there is no other book in the world that achieves this better.*" The comparison he adds is too convincing for me to be reluctant to include it as well: "*To learn Greek antiquities...*"

<summary>The author argues that Hebrew study is a moral and ecclesiastical necessity for theologians, essential for both clear biblical interpretation and missionary work. He introduces several major Rabbinic commentators (such as Rashi and Kimchi) whose works are included in polyglot Bible editions to provide historical and literal context.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that understanding the New Testament (the "New Law") requires deep immersion in Jewish history and Rabbinic literature, just as understanding Roman history requires reading Latin authors.</meta>

...[to learn] <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the necessity of primary sources.</note>, we consult the writers of that nation: no one can teach us the affairs of the Romans better than those who, living in Latium, recorded them in writing. We proceed by the same path to truly examine the customs of other nations, provided some record of them remains in memory. **Now, for the New Law**, knowledge of antiquity and Rabbinic study sheds much light, primarily because CHRIST—about to conduct His persuasion in a human manner, as one among humans—adapted Himself to the character of the people and, so to speak, to their appetite. From this, how great an affinity of style you may find with Rabbinic writing! You find likenesses, the parabolic method of teaching, phraseology <note>original Greek: φρασεολογίαν (phraseologian)</note>, and many parables plucked from the very opinions of the ancients. Thus, that saying by which those who pursue the faults of others are refuted—the speck in the eyes of an offended neighbor while they do not see their own beam—you may find word-for-word in the Mishnah <note>term: Mishnah - the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions, forming the core of the Talmud.</note>: (1) *“In this generation, there is no one who can reprove; if someone says to them, ‘Take the splinter from between your eyes,’ they say to him, ‘Take the beam from between your eyes’”* <note>original Hebrew: בדור הזה אין מי שיכול להוכיח זה אומר להן טול קיסם מבין עיניכם והן אומרין לו טול קורה מבין עיניכם</note>. The prefix <gloss>Tanya</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: תניא (tanya) - a technical term meaning "it was taught" or "we have learned."</note> indicates it is ancient; Rabbi Tarphon <note>A member of the third generation of the Mishnah sages, who lived during the period when the Temple was destroyed.</note> says [it], therefore he himself learned it from elsewhere. He was, however, one of the disciples of Hillel <note>term: Hillel - a famous Jewish sage and founder of a school of thought (House of Hillel) known for its humility and leniency.</note>, and in that dispute between his master and Shammai <note>term: Shammai - the contemporary rival of Hillel, whose school was generally stricter.</note>, he nearly perished—

(1) The Lord’s Prayer itself is inserted almost word-for-word into the modern prayers of the Jews, but it is taken from the tractate *Berakhot* <note>term: Berakhot - the first tractate of the Talmud, focusing on blessings and prayers.</note>; though I would more easily assert that this was silently plucked from the Gospel by the Jews. There are many other things which I wisely omit, lest this grow into a massive volume, which must be constrained within a few leaves. Second, the history of our Savior JESUS CHRIST is protected by nothing more—nor can it be protected—than when you show that such were the rites, customs, and controversies of that time; and from these, many passages will borrow clarity. Do you finally think you understand how it could happen that a twelve-year-old child <note>Refers to the young Jesus in the Temple (Luke 2:41-52).</note> dared to dispute in such an assembly of men and in the temple? You cannot, unless you know that the faculty of debating publicly was open to the ancient Hebrews differently than it is to us. Thus, elsewhere also, a place was given to the arriving Paul: if he had anything to warn the people in the Synagogue, it was permitted. To draw this matter out further would require a longer pen: I am forbidden by my limits to do so. Perhaps you will extricate yourself from this—

<meta>This page continues the argument that New Testament narratives, specifically the judgment of Jesus, are deeply rooted in Jewish legal traditions. The author uses the story of the woman caught in adultery to demonstrate how Rabbinic law (the Mishnah) clarifies Christ’s actions.</meta>

<image-desc>Decorative floral and scrollwork woodcut ornaments at the top of the page.</image-desc>

...judgment, which **CHRIST** delivered regarding the adulteress: in which I was delighted that my own critique <note>original Latin: "crisin meam"</note> was judged as fair by my Father, as if ever before. As John reports (1), a woman caught in adultery is brought forward: the Pharisees ask whether she should be stoned, as the Law of Moses prescribes? **JESUS** says: *He of you who is without sin, let him be the first to cast a stone at her*: they all depart. **CHRIST** dismisses the woman with a warning: *Now, sin no more.* Was she therefore guilty? Why did **CHRIST** rescue her from punishment? Is the crime of another a reason to excuse someone else? Finally, why did they all leave when they heard, *He of you who is without sin*? They did not fear **CHRIST** as a lawmaker; what hidden strength lies in those words? Call upon antiquity: all will become clear. The first throwing of the stone belonged to the witnesses (2) and without witnesses no one—indeed, not even with only one witness—was it lawful to be put to death (3). The Savior wished that some would profess themselves as witnesses: but suitable ones, and those who were without sin, of the kind that makes a person disqualified from testifying <note>original: "intestabilem"</note>: of whom mention is made in the Mishnah (4) <note>original Hebrew: ואלו הן הפסולין המשחק בקוביא והמלוה בריבית ומפריחי יונים וסוחרי שביעית וכו'</note> *these are the unfit witnesses: gamblers, usurers, those who race pigeons (to lure others' birds), and those who trade in the produce of the Sabbatical (seventh) year, etc.* Moreover, there is no giving of 

---

(1) Chapter 8, from verse 3 to 11. (2) Deuteronomy 17, verse 7. (3) The same [chapter], verse 6. (4) Sanhedrin, chapter 3, page 24.

<page-num>74</page-num>

<column-break/>

testimony by one who has once been rejected as unfit, and he is called a <term>rejected witness</term> <note>original Hebrew: עד זומם (ed zomem) - specifically refers to a witness whose testimony is invalidated because they were not present at the scene.</note>. Now to that phrase, *who is without sin*: no one acquitted himself: thus witnesses were lacking: so she was snatched from death, whom it was not lawful to kill without a witness: although **CHRIST** knew she was guilty, and rebuked her with that *sin no more*. 

And how small a part is this of those things which occur everywhere? Thus an argument is often indicated which you would hardly consider an argument, because you are ignorant of other things which were supposed to be known by those to whom they were said; such as that which in chapters two and three was deduced from the <note>original Greek: τοῖς (tois) - likely referring to the specific texts or people mentioned in the Psalms.</note> *The Lord said to my Lord* <note>A reference to Psalm 110:1, frequently used as a Messianic proof in the New Testament.</note>, the force of which is triumphant if it is unraveled. You will also find an argument from the word <term>Cohen</term> <note>Hebrew: כהן (kohen) - Priest.</note>, which is present in Paul regarding the transfer of the priesthood, but he refers to it briefly and for the sake of more abundant knowledge. And with these things, Paul’s letter to the Hebrews is most full; and (in my opinion at least, and what I was taught by my Father) he exhausted all polemic against the Jews; but only with the headings lightly indicated, and so to speak, through summaries. May the loss of such great and divine arguments make us weep! And yet none of us has touched them; not even <term>Lightfoot</term> <note>John Lightfoot (1602–1675), a famous English scholar of Hebrew and Rabbinics whose work "Horae Hebraicae et Talmudicae" sought to explain the New Testament through the Talmud.</note> himself, who once held the "dictatorship" [in this field of study], and let us admit the truth: by his diligence he surpassed his predecessors in this genre, but he deterred his successors, as if one man had already exhausted everything. In these things, however, in vain...

<meta>This page transitions from a legal discussion of witnesses in the New Testament to a broader argument regarding the conversion of the Jewish people. The author emphasizes the need for intellectual engagement and deep cultural knowledge rather than physical force.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographic ornament consisting of three stars and curved flourishes at the top of the text block on both pages.</image-desc>

...so that you may flee to him; these things are indeed more important, or I am mistaken. Finally, I come to that other objective, which is the conversion of the Jews. This must be approached by two paths: for either force is avoided, or it is applied; and this is done either in writing or by verbal debate. Oral debate is conducted either with a learned man or a common person; for these must be attacked from different sides. In general, no one will deny that the Church has retained its right to gather the Jews under the banner of truth, partly by the force of arguments and partly by the grace of internal calling. The harvest is plentiful, and what Tertullian once said of the Christians can surely be proclaimed of this wretched nation: that they fill all our places—cities, islands, forts, towns, meeting places, the forum; they have left only the temples to us. <note>original: "omnia noſtra impleant, urbes inſulas caſtella, municipia, conciliabula, forum; templa nobis ſola reliquerunt." This is a famous quote from Tertullian's *Apology*, Chapter 37, originally describing the rapid growth of the early Church.</note> But in such a vast harvest, how few are the workers! Indeed, what need is there (though this too is necessary) to first cross all the seas and penetrate beyond the shivering Caucasus, when such a great multitude surrounds us to whom we might announce the truth? Surely that word of Paul to the Romans must be fulfilled: *Blindness in part has happened to Israel, until the fullness of the Gentiles has come in*; and this fullness has entered. If you object that they are stubborn: all the more effort must be made to break them. But how could this be said of [Christ] himself? There were certainly not many who could break the bread for those who hungered for it. As for those who were there—truly they did not regret the labor! Jerome of Santa

<column-break/>

Santa Fide <note>Jerome of Santa Fide (formerly Joshua Lorki) was a 15th-century Spanish physician and convert from Judaism who became a prominent polemicist against his former faith.</note> must be brought forward, not in my own words, but in those which are added toward the end of both his books, which are inserted in Volume IV of the *Library of the Holy Fathers* in the address to the reader. *Take note, reader, that in the year 1412, in the month of August, this useful book against the treachery of the Jews was compiled by the Venerable Master Jerome of Santa Fide, formerly a Jew, by whose recitation more than five thousand Jews were converted to the faith.* How many thousands did Martini lead to his side with that *Dagger of Faith*? <note>Referring to the *Pugio Fidei*, a famous 13th-century polemical work by the Dominican friar Raymund Martini which used Rabbinic texts to argue for Christian truths.</note> It is a book in which so many things are contained that one might even wish to suspend judgment as to whether that work is truly his. But whoever the author may be, there are many things in it that serve as arguments. My only wish is that he had maintained a better, or at least a sharper and more pressing, logic. Indeed, you might grieve that many excellent passages lie dormant, cold, and like mangled limbs and bones, awaiting Ezekiel the restorer. <note>A reference to the biblical vision of the Valley of Dry Bones in Ezekiel 37, where the prophet is told to breathe life back into the remains of the house of Israel.</note> I have wandered a little from the path; I return from where I turned aside, and I say: those who ever wish to engage in the battle with the Jews (and many surely ought to wish it) must first of all be armed with an intimate knowledge of Hebrew affairs <term>Ebraicarum rerum cognitione: an understanding of Jewish law, scripture, and tradition</term>. Look at some great military leader, such as our own times provide; before all else, he investigates where the greatest strength and the "citadel of fortitude" lies in the enemy. Then, he searches for the flank most exposed to a blow, and strikes in that part—

<meta>This page continues the author's argument on the necessity of deep scholarship for the conversion of the Jewish people. Having established the obligation to preach, the author now addresses the practical difficulties of debate and the psychological mindset of the opponent.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring floral scrolls and three stars.</image-desc>

...to come. You have an idea of what a combatant must do: both the strength and the weakness of the enemy must be discerned; and from where will you discern these things, except from their own books? If authority is needed for persuasion, hear **Lactantius** (1) concerning a certain man in Bithynia who, in his own time, disputed with a philosopher; where, says Lactantius, *when he wished to confirm the reasoning of the religion against which he was speaking, he appeared foolish, empty, and ridiculous.* Why? *Because that serious advisor of others' utility not only did not know what he was attacking, but did not even know what he was saying.* <note>original: "gravis ille conſultor utilitatis alienæ non modo quid oppugnaret, ſed quid loqueretur, neſciebat." This quote emphasizes that one cannot effectively argue against a belief without understanding it first.</note> You will experience the same thing if you enter the fray unprepared, thinking it sufficient if you thrust at the Jew the verse, *the scepter shall not depart from Judah, etc.* <note>A reference to Genesis 49:10, a traditional "proof text" used by Christians to argue that the Messiah had already come since Jewish self-governance had ended.</note> He laughs—and why? Truly, as one about to attack, you must know what you are attacking, and then with what weapons. For what makes the battle with the **Hebrew** <term>Hebrew: Here used to refer to a Jewish person as a theological opponent.</term> most difficult is this: he does not desire to bring us over to his side at all; he considers it enough if he can ward off force and defend himself. Our cause is different, for we are commanded to *preach the Gospel to every creature,* a command that still endures. This discrepancy between the two, as well as the antiquity of his own sect, causes him to contend that he is "in possession," as the saying goes; therefore, he conducts the whole matter by denial. And because he is not anxious about convincing us, he demands that faith be placed in all his own traditions, while he dismisses each of ours. Nor should the assertion of **Augustin Calmet** <note>Augustin Calmet (1672–1757) was a famous French Benedictine monk and biblical scholar known for his extensive commentaries.</note> deceive you,

<margin>(1) Divine Institutes, Book 5, chapter 2.</margin>

---

<page-num>7</page-num>
<meta>The catchword "ne" from the previous page begins the word "nempe" (namely) in the following section, though the OCR shows it appearing after the Calmet reference.</meta>
