when in his dissertation on the Characteristics of the **Messiah**, he says: *at the very least, they ought to agree to grant the Gospel that degree of authority which they give to other ancient histories.* With all due respect to those who defer to this man, I say: he is mistaken. Do you know what the Jew thinks of the Gospel? Stop your ears against blasphemy and fortify your heart against indignation: Forgive me, O Savior, that I bring forth this most impious assertion with which they harass the testimonies of Your Divinity! They claim, namely, that the narrative is a fabrication, and was adapted to prophecies not by the actual event of things, but by cunning—which is abundantly clear from that pestilential *Fortification of Faith*, which is the "third fiery dart of Satan," as **Wagenseil** <note>Johann Christoph Wagenseil (1633–1705) was a Christian Hebraist who published a collection of Jewish polemics titled "Tela Ignea Satanae" (The Fiery Darts of Satan) in 1681 to help Christians refute them.</note> labels it with an appropriate name. What then remains for you? That he be convinced from his own books. First, the sinister opinions conceived about Christians must be uprooted; then they must be refuted and pierced with their own weapons: let the authority of the ancients weaken the moderns, and let the conflict of the ancients among themselves enfeeble them. Thus you will make progress; for even the most foolish Jew is clever and slippery. But he certainly understands this: if you bring forward opposing arguments face to face, both cannot be true; one, therefore, is false, and so his authority fails. And this is a great deal, indeed very much; for as **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> says somewhere, *a sick man has hope for health when he realizes he is sick.* <note>original: "tunc ægroto ſpes valetudinis, cum ſe ſentit ægrotare."</note> But will you accomplish these things without the help of those studies? And truly not—

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion on the necessary scholarship for debating Jewish theologians. The previous page established that one must understand the opponent's books to avoid looking foolish. Here, the author lists the great Christian Hebraists who have paved the way, while warning against relying solely on secondary summaries rather than the primary Jewish sources.</meta>

Is it not a necessity to perform these tasks—by which I mean, to convince and expose the Jews? But others have already performed this, having prepared materials from which you might draw: the scholarly works of **Raymond Martin**, **Petrus Galatinus**, **Paul Fagius**, **Sebastian Münster**, **Scaliger**, **Baronius**, both the elder and younger **Buxtorf**, **Grotius**, **Schickard**, **Drusius**, **Cunaeus**, both the elder and younger **Cappell**, **Cameron**, **Sixtinus Amama**, **Petavius**, **Selden**, **Vorstius**, **Hottinger**, **Hackspan**, **Schnell**, **Cocceius**, **Morin**, **Wagenseil**, **Bossuet**, and many others, including most recently our very close friend **Parfusius**. So many, I say, and more besides, have prepared these things so that you may use them when the need arises. 

Therefore, would you rather lean on the labors of others than investigate anything yourself? Whoever says this, I must rouse you with an insult, the same one **Cicero** shouted at those like you in the second book of *On the Orator*: *It is the mark of a slow mind to follow trickling streams and not see the sources of things; it is the mark of our age and experience to summon what we want from the head [the source] and to see where everything flows from.* <note>Original Latin: "Tardi ingenii est rivulos consectari, fontes rerum non videre; aetatis est ususque nostri à capite quod velimus arcessere, & unde omnia manant videre."</note> Certainly, it is the mark of an overly confident mind, and one prone to being deceived. How many insincere, altered, or mutilated citations from Martin did my father point out to me? Thus all trust is lost if you hurl such things at a Jew—at least one who is eager to escape your grasp. What then? They confuse the writings of any common person with those who are eminent, without distinction, and they seek arguments from sources where the Jew himself admits they are merely "old wives' tales."

<column-break/>

How often has **Eisenmenger** stirred the appetite in his *Judaism Unmasked* <note>original: "Judaïsmo detecto." This was a famous anti-Jewish work by Johann Andreas Eisenmenger, published around 1700.</note>, a work especially notable for this fault: but he wanted his book to be large rather than good. We must be entirely careful of this, lest it be said that <term>a dwarf is sent to defeat a giant</term> <note>Original: "ננס נגד ענק" (nanos neged anaq). This suggests that an ill-equipped scholar attempting to refute the central Jewish texts is like a dwarf attacking a giant.</note>. 

Therefore, the most important opinion is that which one brings directly from the **Talmud** <term>Talmud: The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing the discussions of ancient rabbis on law, ethics, and tradition.</term>. Among the Talmudic scholars, they enjoy this hierarchy: <note>Original Hebrew: "גדול מרבי רבן . וגדול מרבן שמו" (Gadol me-Rebbi Rabban, ve-gadol me-Rabban shemo).</note> *Greater than a "Rabbi" is a "Rabban," and greater than a "Rabban" is his own name* (when used alone, indicating the highest stature). Moreover, they succeed each other thus: the **Thanaim** <term>Thanaim: The "Repeaters" or teachers of the oral law during the first two centuries of the Common Era.</term> or traditional doctors; the **Amoraim** <term>Amoraim: The "Speakers" or interpreters who commented on the teachings of the Thanaim.</term> who speak of these things with authority; and the **Gaonim** <term>Gaonim: Meaning "Geniuses," these were the heads of the great Jewish academies in Babylonia.</term>, who possess I know not what power from the laying on of hands. 

Following them are **Rabbi Alphas** <note>Isaac Alfasi (1013–1103), a major legal scholar.</note>, **Our Teacher Jacob, Master of the Rows** <note>Jacob ben Asher (c. 1269–1343), author of the Arba'ah Turim, a seminal code of Jewish law.</note>, and so on. Closely related to these are **Maimonides** <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), the famous philosopher and codifier.</note> and his contemporaries <note>original Greek: "σύγχρονοι" (synchronoi)</note> **Ibn Ezra** and **Jarchi** <note>Jarchi is an old scholarly name for Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent commentator on the Bible and Talmud.</note>. And just as each is closer in age to the source, so he is closer in authority. **Augustin Calmet** has woven together the order and history of the more recent scholars in his *Dissertation on the Schools of the Hebrews*, and quite diligently too. Unless you observe these distinctions, you fight in the manner of the **andabatae** <term>Andabatae: Ancient gladiators who fought blindfolded or in helmets without eye-holes.</term>, striking out with useless blows.

Now someone might lament to me the loss of time spent on books of this kind, in which stories of deceit and fabrication are perpetually interwoven—as **Saint Jerome** is cited as a witness (1), and another cites **Forster**...

---

(1) Epistle to Algasia, questions 9 and 10; and on Isaiah, chapter 8.

...someone objects, who at the beginning of the preface which he prefixed to his lexicon, openly and solemnly testifies: *"that in the commentaries of the Rabbis there is no light, no knowledge of God, no spirit, no true and solid knowledge of any discipline or art, no knowledge of languages, and much less of the Hebrew language."* <note>original: "in commentariis Rabinorum nullam esse lucem, nullam notitiam DEI, nullum spiritum, nullum ullius disciplinae seu artis, veram ac solidam scientiam, nullam lingvarum, ac nedum Ebreae lingvae cognitionem"</note> This is a sufficient warning, you might say, from a man who dared to profess about himself in that same preface: *"If there is anyone who has poured his resources into the Rabbis, who has spent a long time in their synagogues, who has supported their teachers at home at his own expense, who has studiously examined their commentaries, it is Forster,"* <note>Johann Forster (1496–1556) was a German Lutheran theologian and Hebraist.</note> and yet (he adds) *"I call God and my conscience to witness, I have brought back nothing extraordinary, nothing worthy of singular praise."* <note>original: "si quis est, qui facultates suas in Rabbinos profudit... Forsterus est... DEUM & conscientiam testor, nihil eximii, quod singulari laude dignum reportavi."</note> A harsh saying; especially to me, who not So many, I say, and more besides, are available for you to use their work whenever the need arises.

Therefore, would you rather lean on the labor of others than investigate anything yourself? To whoever says this, I must respond with the same reproach with which **Cicero** harassed those like him in the second book of *On the Orator*: *"It is the mark of a slow mind to follow trickling streams and not see the sources of things; it is the mark of our age and experience to summon what we want from the source and to see where everything flows from."* <note>original: "Tardi ingenii est rivulos consectari, fontes rerum non videre; aetatis est ususque nostri à capite quod velimus arcessere, & unde omnia manant videre."</note> Certainly, this is the mark of an overly confident mind, and one that is easily deceived. How many insincere, altered, or mutilated citations from Mar...

<meta>This page continues the argument in favor of Rabbinic studies. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how one might use these texts as "weapons" and "shields" against critics. The catchword "num" leads into the phrase "adversariorum impetum" (the attack of adversaries).</meta>

->* * *<-

[Maimonides] <note>The text likely refers to Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), a pivotal Jewish philosopher who codified Jewish law.</note> condensed a summary of the **Mishnah** <note>The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions, forming the core of the Talmud.</note>. Examine it: you will change your opinion; you will admit there are many useful things there, and you will clear the Mishnah itself of the fables, many passages of which are found in the **Gemara** <note>The component of the Talmud comprising rabbinical analysis and commentary on the Mishnah.</note>. **Wagenseil** <note>Johann Christoph Wagenseil (1633–1705), a Christian Hebraist who sought to understand Jewish texts, sometimes for polemical but often for scholarly reasons.</note> champions the study of the **Talmud** in his preface, and he relieves us of that burden, which he, in his great erudition, has performed most excellently. I add one thing: that many of the traditions contained in the Mishnah are sincere, as **Augustin Calmet** <note>A French Benedictine monk (1672–1757) known for his Bible commentaries.</note> judges in his dissertation on the Schools of the Hebrews: *true rays of genuine tradition (he says) shine through, etc.* You ask for an example? Look at Paul! (1) regarding the receiving of the Sinai law: *“Moses said: I am terrified and trembling”*: where is this? It is nowhere in the **Pentateuch** <note>The first five books of the Bible (Torah).</note>; yet in the Talmud (2) a fuller history is preserved; I transcribe it here:

*When Moses ascended on high (to Heaven), the ministering spirits said: "What is one born of a woman doing among us?" etc. Moses said: "I am afraid, lest they consume me with the breath of their mouths."* <note>original: "כשעלה משה למרום אמרו מלאכי השרת מה לילוד אשה בינינו וכו אמר משה מתירא אני שמא ישרפוני בהבל פיהם"</note>

This fragment shows—indeed, it proves—that these traditions are not to be held as nothing, for so great an Apostle recognized their value. Let us also recognize it, when we seek to obtain from them arrows and quivers for attacking, and shields for repelling the insane

<margin>
(1) See Hebrews chapter 12, verse 21. (2) In the Gemara, Tractate Shabbat, page 87.
</margin>

<column-break/>

->* * *<-

attack and assault of adversaries; for it is both permitted and helpful to seek decisive weapons for victory from those sources. I wish that these things would persuade and convince you, not merely by my words, but by the facts. Meet the desire of the **Most August Empress** <note>Likely referring to Maria Theresa of Austria, the reigning monarch at the time of writing in Vienna.</note> with the same kindness with which she has always met your desires; and by your very eagerness to receive this, show that you would have asked for it, even as you receive it.

Since my Father no longer has the opportunity to address you, Kind Reader, he has commanded me to signify to you in his name: that however great this little book may be, you owe it to the *Most Reverend and Most Ample Lord, the Prelate of the Sacred and Exempt Order of Canons Regular of the Royal Church of Louka in Moravia* <note>The Louka Abbey (Kloster Bruck) was a significant Premonstratensian monastery.</note>, who, out of his munificence toward the literate, provided both the permission and the expenses for its printing. My father asks that you favor his efforts: that by your approval, you might bring forth the publication of those things for which he has pledged his word; and he asks that you taste the use of Rabbinic Philology from these pages, as if at a first table. As for me, if I have stumbled anywhere, I shall easily obtain pardon if you consider that I am one who, by reason of age, may yet be restored to wholeness. To please you is beyond my prayer; I am satisfied not to displease. Judge this little work thus: the good parts belong to my Parent, and the flaws must be imputed to my own weakness. I was writing this in **Vienna** <note>original: "Augustæ Vindobonensi"</note>: in the year of the Christian era <term>1759</term> <note>original: "MDCCLIX"</note>, on the 5th day before the Kalends of July <note>June 27, 1759.</note>.

# INTRODUCTION <note>original: "ΕΙΣΑΓΩΓΗ"</note>

It was not with a new madness, nor was he the first to do so by his own impulse, that **Photinus** <note>Photinus (d. 376 AD) was a bishop of Sirmium whose teachings, known as Photinianism, denied the pre-existence and full divinity of Christ, arguing he was a mere man who became divine.</note> (he whose impious dogma the **First Council of Constantinople** <note>Held in 381 AD, this council expanded the Nicene Creed and specifically condemned various heresies, including those of Photinus.</note>—the second among the ecumenical councils—condemned with anathemas) dared to attack the **Divinity of CHRIST**. The Jewish sect contemporary to our Savior cherished in common that same sacrilegious mindset concerning the **MESSIAH**; but this [distinction exists] between the errors of both...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the formal introduction, where the author links the 4th-century heresy of Photinus (denial of Christ's divinity) to the views of Jewish sects during the time of Jesus.</summary>
<keywords>Photinus, Divinity of Christ, Council of Constantinople, Messiah, Heresy, Jewish Sects</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Introduction," contrasting the heresy of Photinus with the views of Jewish contemporaries of Jesus. While Photinus accepted the spiritual nature of Christ's mission but denied His divinity, the author argues that the Jewish leaders of that time expected a purely physical, political liberation and were largely ignorant of the mystery of the Messiah's divine nature. The catchword "tit" from the previous page's "stetit" (he stood) connects the bottom of page 2 to the top of page 3.</meta>

distinction: that he <note>Photinus</note> did not deny the universal and spiritual salvation brought to the human race by **CHRIST**; but those others <note>the Jewish leaders and people of that era</note>—at least the more prominent among them—believed salvation was for the twelve tribes alone. They expected nothing more than a savior who would strike the infamous yoke of slavery from their necks and lead them back to Canaan from their dispersal across every shore of the world. In a word, they judged that a merely physical redemption would be performed by the **MESSIAH**.

If we direct our attention more closely, we may perceive from this that a certain mystery had been withdrawn from the knowledge of most people: namely, that he who was to work the salvation of **Israel** would be at once man and at once **GOD**. And truly, if anyone doubts this concealment, I would have him examine the words of the high priest to **CHRIST** and be convinced. There stood

<column-break/>

<page-num>3</page-num>

the most innocent one before the council, attacked indeed from all sides, but in vain; for they could allege nothing against Him for which He might be blamed, even though *many false witnesses had come forward*. Then, as if weaving a trap, the High Priest <note>Caiaphas</note> asks: was **CHRIST** the **SON OF GOD**? And suddenly, when **JESUS** had replied, "*you have said it*" <note>A common Hebraic way of saying "Yes."</note>, [the priest] tearing his clothes, exclaims: "*He has blasphemed! why do we still need witnesses? Look! Now you have heard his blasphemy.*" (1)

I ask: would the High Priest have dared to accuse **CHRIST** of blasphemy before the crowd of people standing by if it had been common knowledge among everyone that the one who was to redeem **Israel** must necessarily be **GOD**, or the **Son of GOD**? Let someone else dare to affirm that; I certainly will not.

I am not alone among the more sensible in this view. Rather, because the degree to which he was destined for the completion of such a great work was kept secret from the common people, it was easy for the envy of the priests to turn this into a crime: to rouse the crowd against **CHRIST** on the suspicion of a crime as if it were openly admitted, and to attack His mission using this as a sort of proof.

And the Scripture, read before the assembly of the people, could seem to be no small supporter of these envious attempts, for nowhere does it predict anything <term>explicitly</term> <note>original: ῥητῶς (rhētōs)</note> and clearly regarding the Divinity of the **MESSIAH**. What, then, was the reason for concealing such a great matter, and one so necessary to be known? We believe it lies in this: it is the constant opinion of all theologians that the profound <term>mystery</term> <note>original: μυσήριον (mystērion)</note> of the Holy Trinity had to be hidden from the Hebrew people, lest, if it were revealed (since they were, drawn

---

<page-num>5</page-num>
<sig>A 3</sig>

drawn from the contagion of Egypt and its neighbors, and prone to idolatry) they might seize upon such a subtle doctrine, and, misunderstanding it, derive <term>polytheism</term> <note>original: πολυθέϊαν (polytheian)</note> from it. Why should a similar reason not have persuaded them not to make manifest the divinity of the coming Redeemer? Since it was feared that <term>polytheism</term> <note>original: πολυθέϊα (polytheia)</note> might creep in by that same path, as that doctrine would have exceeded the grasp of any common person—the very source from which quite a few heresies have sprouted for us as well.

Therefore, I suspect (and I believe I am not mistaken) that knowledge of this was among the secrets (*) entrusted only to the wiser men; and I am strongly persuaded to suspect this by various writings of the ancient Hebrews

<margin>(*) A similar *discipline of the secret* <note>The historical practice in the early Church of keeping certain doctrines and rituals, like the Eucharist, hidden from non-believers and catechumens.</note> also held sway in the Church during the first two, or rather three, centuries when it was pressed by persecutions; *Samuel van Schelstrate* wrote learnedly on this subject against *Tentzel*.</margin>

so something must be offered to those men as a kind of bait until they are made fully teachable. Indeed, they will also be grieved. For that long-standing opinion, deeply implanted in the minds of many, has always **stirred my stomach** <note>A Latin idiom for causing indignation or irritation.</note>: as if every single one of the **Hebrews** clearly knew the **Christian truth** and were held in their error by **stubbornness** <note>original: *pertinacia*. A common historical trope used to describe the Jewish refusal to convert.</note> alone. Suppose there were a reward for deceiving: I would not disagree. But by what hope, I ask you, are these miserable people encouraged in that stubbornness of theirs, bearing nothing but hardships, insults, and contempt? Would they prefer to be despised and enslaved for a mere error, renouncing the comforts of this life and—as if they were well aware—losing the happiness of the next as well? Therefore, judge it thus: they do not know their own **darkness**, which seems to them like light; just as for those who dwell in dark places, their own shadows do not seem as opaque as they appear to another who goes from the light into the dark. I say it again: they are ignorant of themselves; they must be taught and convinced. While this...

<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory remarks regarding his methodology. The previous page discussed the common Christian misconception that Jewish people remained in their faith solely out of stubbornness. The author here proposes a more intellectual approach to persuasion.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<meta>catchword: BOOK</meta>

...[stubbornness which] we labor to address in the present work, we have set this rule for ourselves: we will bring forth arguments from the **Old Law** <term>Lex Vetus</term> <note>The Hebrew Bible or Mosaic Law.</note>: but especially we will demonstrate the invincible strength of those arguments with which the Lord JESUS refuted the **experts of the law** <note>original: *legis peritos*. A reference to the scribes and lawyers in the New Testament who specialized in interpreting the Torah.</note>. Alongside these, we will place the corresponding Rabbinic interpretation—or at least that which favors our cause—in which we always maintain the same order that we taught in the preface must not be neglected when dealing with them. Finally, by this method, we will lead those who have been **Convicted** <note>In this context, "convicted" means those who have been intellectually persuaded or overcome by the weight of the evidence.</note> to the clearer truth of the gospel; there they may recognize the fulfillment of what we have demonstrated must certainly be achieved, as well as how, by whom, and at what time.

# BOOK ONE
->CONCERNING THE<-
# DIVINITY OF THE MESSIAH,
->HIS<-
# NAMES, WORKS,

### CHANGE OF THE LAW.

| **Chapter V.** | The works of the MESSIAH: the victory over the Devil; satisfaction for **original sin** <note>original: *culpa primitiva*.</note>; the change of the law; and the reason for this change, once again from the confessions of the Rabbis. |

## CHAPTER I.
### *Certain preliminary questions concerning the Messiah.*

...brews (the greater part of them) imagine. A man (they say) will be born from the lineage of David, a King, such as no other has ever been; a Ruler, who will lead the remnants of the tribes back into the promised land, restore the temple, and re-establish the sacrifices. He will be terrifying to his neighbors, exalt his nation beyond measure, and hold all the kings of the world as his subjects <note>original: *vectigales*, referring to those who pay tribute or taxes</note>. (1) I wisely pass over what other trifles these fools babble, and how they flatter themselves by misinterpreting Isaiah: such as the idea that individual kings will have footmen, and queens for nurses, and whatever other fables they fertilely devise. Yet all these things indicate one point: that they wait only for a physical liberation <note>original: *corporalem... liberationem*</note>. Come now! let us raise certain objections that will challenge the strength of their arguments.

I. When we turn the pages of scripture, we find them filled with the sighs of those longing for the MESSIAH. To take a few examples from the heap, I will begin with Jacob, who, while blessing his sons before his departure, exclaims with a clear break in the thread of his speech <note>Greek: *akatallēlótēs*, a rhetorical term for a sudden grammatical or logical inconsistency</note>: *I wait for your salvation, O LORD!* <note>Hebrew: *Li-yshu’at-kha qivviti Yahweh*</note> (1). This passage is explained by the Paraphrast Jonathan <note>Referring to Targum Jonathan, an ancient Aramaic translation and commentary on the Hebrew Bible</note> as the MESSIAH, the son of David, for whom the Patriarch yearned. Balaam also marvels at him (2): *I see him, but not now; I behold him, but not near.* Indeed, the Paraphrasts Jonathan and Onkelos <note>Onkelos is the author of the most famous Aramaic Targum of the Torah</note> interpret this: *when a King shall arise from Jacob, and the MESSIAH shall be exalted from Israel.* <note>Aramaic: *kad yaqum malka mi-Ya’akov ve-yitrabba Meshicha mi-Yisrael*</note>. Consider also that saying of Moses from his song (3) which from...

II. It is worth marveling at the opinion of the Rabbis themselves regarding the MESSIAH. An Egyptian <note>Likely referring to Maimonides (1138–1204), the famous Jewish philosopher and jurist who lived in Egypt.</note> relates a passage excerpted from the tractate Sanhedrin: (1) <note>original Hebrew: "אלו הן שאין להם חלק לעולם הבא . אלא נכרתין ואובדין ונדונין על גורל רשעים . וחטאתם לעולם ולעולמי עולמים . האפיקורסין . והכופרים בתורה . והכופרים בתחיית המתים ובביאת הגואל ."</note> *These are those who have no portion in the world to come, but are cut off, perish, and are judged with the lot of the wicked, and their sin remains forever and ever: the Epicureans <note>A term used in Rabbinic literature for heretics or those who treat the Law with contempt.</note>; those who deny the Law; and those who deny the resurrection of the dead and the coming of the Redeemer.* In Sanhedrin, the number of these is also set down. This very point, as an article of belief among the XIII

For what prerogative does this MESSIAH have over others? [Is it possible that] the rejection of a mere man could bring with it the loss of eternal life! Maimonides sensed that this testimony might be difficult; therefore, he strives to moderate it, and in another place adds the reason: (2) <note>original Hebrew: "כל מי שאינו מאמין או מחכה לביאתו (לביאת המשיח) לא בשאר נביאים בלבד הוא כופר . אלא אף בתורה ובמשה רבינו"</note> *Anyone who does not believe in or wait for his coming (the coming of the Messiah) denies not only the other prophets, but also the Law and our teacher Moses.*

Our Teacher <note>referring to Maimonides</note>. For behold, the Torah testified concerning him. <note>original Hebrew: "רבינו . שהרי התורה העידה עליו ."</note> *Whoever does not believe, or does not hope for his (the MESSIAH'S) coming, denies not only the remaining prophets but also the Law and Moses; since the Law testifies concerning him.* <note>original: "Quisquis non credit, aut non sperat ejusdem (MESSIÆ) adventum, non tantum negat reliquos prophetas, sed & legem & Mosem; quoniam lex de eo testatur."</note> I do not accept this reasoning, for two reasons. First, if this loss of the life to come is imminent because the Law—in which his coming is foretold—is denied: then there is no difference between this and any other commandment. For the same reason, anyone who does not apply phylacteries <note>Small leather boxes containing Torah verses worn during prayer; original: "phylacteria"</note>, or sets aside the four-cornered garment with hanging threads <note>The "tallit" with its ritual fringes or "tzitzit."</note>, or who eats forbidden animals, would have no portion in the world to come. Secondly, the <term>Mischne</term> <note>The *Mishneh Torah*, Maimonides' comprehensive code of Jewish law.</note> would have committed a <term>tautology</term> <note>original: "ταυτολογίαν" (tautologian); the fault of repeating the same thing in different words.</note>; for if the denial of the MESSIAH causes the life to come to be lost because it is simultaneously a denial of the Law, it would certainly already be contained in the third category regarding the denial of the Law; why was it necessary to list it as something unique, with the addition of a

<column-break/>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: tur-</meta>

fourth number to be repeated? Yet they would not dare to admit a <term>tautology</term> <note>original: "ταυτολογίαν"</note> in this symbolic book of theirs; it remains, therefore, that such severe threats, by which they are driven to have faith in the coming of the MESSIAH, must draw their origin from elsewhere.

III. No sane person will deny that the redemption to be fulfilled through the MESSIAH—whatever its nature may eventually be, even if merely physical—must be greater and more glorious than that Egyptian redemption of which Moses was the instrument. Compare them, if you dare to doubt. In the latter <note>The Exodus from Egypt.</note>, the people to be led out were all together: six hundred thousand men, each capable of battle—a force that seemed able to claim its liberty by its own strength. But in the former <note>The Messianic redemption.</note>, the scattered remnants of the tribes, dispersed into whatever regions, can scarcely be numbered; they are a fearful troop, and long...

<summary>The author argues that belief in the Messiah must be a distinct and primary dogma in Jewish thought, rather than just a sub-point of believing in the Law of Moses. He also begins a comparison between the Exodus from Egypt and the future Messianic redemption, suggesting the latter must be more miraculous given the current scattered state of the Jewish people.</summary>

*“and I will strike every firstborn of Egypt.”* (1) The Talmudic Rabbis <note>The ancient Jewish scholars who compiled the Talmud.</note> explain: **I, and not an emissary; I, and not an angel; I, and not a seraph.** <note>original Hebrew: "אני ולא שליח . אני ולא מלאך : אני ולא שרף" (Ani velo shaliach, ani velo malach, ani velo saraf).</note> (2) *I (says the Lord), I myself, and no messenger; I, not an angel; I, no Seraphim.* And this final [redemption]—more perfect, more powerful, more celebrated, and more difficult, even if it were only temporal <note>Meaning "of this world" or "physical," as opposed to eternal/spiritual.</note>—shall a man work it? From these points set forth, I thus conclude: if the <term>redemption</term> <note>The act of being saved or delivered from a state of bondage.</note> of the MESSIAH is purely *physical* and from servitude, why did Jacob long for it? Why did he not rather long for liberation from the Egyptian servitude? Why would David and Solomon long for it—who were never slaves, and who lacked for neither abundance nor honor? If it is *spiritual*...

It will certainly seem a hard thing to a Jewish person to believe that he is GOD, whom he also knows to be a man, and who, according to the promise, was to be descended from the seed of David. We shall therefore be occupied in this task: to vindicate the divinity of the MESSIAH using the ancient Law and the confessions of the Rabbis.

## CHAPTER II.

->*Christ’s argument from that passage of Psalm 110: The Lord said to my Lord.*<-

Let the first and foremost argument for the Divinity <note>original: "Deitate"</note> of the MESSIAH be that of CHRIST (1) taken from the 110th Psalm. If this seems to some to be less than compelling, they truly reveal that, like people with bleary eyes treated with ointment <note>A classical metaphor for someone who is unable to see the obvious truth despite having the means to do so.</note>, they do not grasp its power. O Savior, stir my mind and pen! Your honor is at stake—You who, as the uncreated wisdom of the Father, knew best how those who insulted Your Divinity were to be convinced. Christ asks in the place cited: *“What do you think about the CHRIST? Whose son is he?”* They say to him: *“David’s.”* He says to them: *“How then does David in the spirit call him Lord, saying: The Lord said...”*

---
(1) Matthew, chapter 22, verse 42 etc., and Luke, chapter 20.

He begins an analysis of Psalm 110, where King David refers to the Messiah as "Lord," implying a status higher than a mere human descendant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s analysis of Psalm 110, specifically focusing on the debate between Jesus and the Pharisees regarding the Messiah’s lineage and divinity. It examines medieval Jewish commentaries from Rashi (Jarchi) and Ibn Ezra (Aben Esra) that attempt to interpret the "Lord" in the psalm as someone other than the divine Messiah.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<meta>catchword: pli-</meta>

...Lord <note>The Latin text begins with "-nus," completing the word "Dominus" from the previous page's catchword.</note> *to my Lord, sit at my right hand, etc. If David therefore calls him son, how is he his Lord?* and (the Gospel writer <note>original: "evangeliographus"</note> continues) *they were not able to answer him a word.* The Pharisees were forced into silence, struck by such a powerful lightning-bolt of truth; for them, it was beyond controversy that the cited passage of David was to be understood as referring to the MESSIAH; although in explaining it, the more recent Rabbis, with whom we are dealing, struggle in various ways and try entirely to attach a different sense to those words of David. Two primary opinions—those of Jarchi <note>Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French commentator known as Rashi.</note> and Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish scholar and biblical commentator.</note>—are accepted by modern scholars. Let us see where they lead. Jarchi claims that the Psalmist, in the words *to my Lord*, understood Abraham, whom he followed with this mention of honor as his ancestor. Aben Esra helps us avoid subverting this ap-

---

<page-num>27</page-num>
<meta>catchword: והנה</meta>

-plication <note>Completing the word "applicatione" from the previous page.</note> and tears it down by comparing the context of the following verse: *The Lord shall send the rod of your strength out of Zion.* In the time of Abraham, Zion did not yet exist; therefore, he rightly concludes, the speech of the king could not have been directed toward him. What then does Aben Esra offer instead? He says this psalm was composed by David’s singers, and thus "to my Lord" was spoken to David himself. But the psalm continues to address the same person in one breath! Was it then also sworn to David in the fourth verse <note>original: "אתה כהן לעולם על דברתי מלכי צדק" — Psalm 110:4</note>: *you are a priest forever according to the order of Melchizedek?* He does not hesitate to assert this, twisting the application of individual words with violence. I record his words here: <note>original: "כל כהן משרת . וכן ובני דוד כהנים היו . והטעם לעבוד השם . והנה"</note> "Every priest is a servant. And likewise, 'the sons of David were priests.' <note>A reference to 2 Samuel 8:18, which Ibn Ezra uses to argue that the term "priest" can mean a royal official.</note> And the meaning is 'to serve the Name' [God]. And behold..."

<summary>This page critiques medieval Jewish interpretations of Psalm 110, arguing against Rashi's view that it refers to Abraham and Ibn Ezra's view that it refers to King David, maintaining instead that it must refer to the divine Messiah.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 110, Rashi, Ibn Ezra, Melchizedek, Zion, Davidic Lineage, Messianic Prophecy, Pharisees</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Psalm 110, specifically analyzing Abraham ibn Ezra’s (Aben Esra) interpretation. The focus shifts to the definition of the word "Cohen" (priest) and whether it refers to a secular minister or a religious priest in the context of David and Melchizedek.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<meta>catchword: hen</meta>

And behold, the reason is that Israel will fight, and you will take the tithe, just as Melchizedek took it from Abraham. <note>original Hebrew: והנה הטעם שילחמו ישראל ואתה תקח המעשר . כאשר לקח מלכי צדק מאברהם</note>
Every **priest** (*cohen*) denotes a **minister**: so that in the book of Samuel (1) the sons of David were *cohanim* <note>Hebrew for priests; see 2 Samuel 8:18</note>, whose purpose was to serve GOD. Therefore, the sense is: that the Israelites will fight, and you, David! will receive tithes, just as Melchizedek received them from Abraham. I pause here: did he not knowingly substitute the word *minister* because it is ambiguous? Nevertheless, it admits no other meaning than *priest*. The passage itself proves this, as it contains pure promises, and thus points to what is essential in each individual word. The word <term>mesharet (משרת): a servant or attendant</term> itself proves it. "Minister" is a familiar usage for Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a Spanish-Jewish scholar</note>, inasmuch as he brings it forward to explain such texts in which by the word *priest* (*co-*

---
(1) 2 Samuel chapter 8, verse 18.

<column-break/>

<page-num>29</page-num>
-hen) <note>original: "co-hen"</note> undoubtedly a **priest** is signified. Thus, he comments on the words *priest of On* (1): *Either a servant of the Name [God] or a servant of an idol; for there is no "cohen" in the whole Scripture except for a "minister" in my opinion.* <note>original Hebrew: או עובד השם . או עובד עבודה זרה . כי אין כהן בכל המקרא כי אם משרת לפי דעתי</note> This "priest of On" means he was either a priest of GOD or of idols, since the word *cohen* throughout the course of scripture denotes a minister. And while the holy text addresses the father-in-law of Moses as the *priest of Midian* (2), he writes: *Every "cohen" in the Bible is a minister, whether to the Name or to an idol.* <note>original Hebrew: כל כהן שבמקרא משרת הוא . לשם . או לעבודה זרה</note> As often as the word *cohen* is read in the Bible, it indicates either a minister of the Lord or of idols. In both places, where he himself substituted the [term] minister <note>original Greek: τὸ minister</note>, neither he nor anyone else contends that anything else is signified than

---
(1) Genesis chapter 41, verse 45. (2) Exodus chapter 2, verse 16.

than *priest* (*) That this is also to be understood in the present psalm is excellently confirmed by the passage in Samuel, to which Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a Spanish-Jewish scholar and grammarian</note> refers: there, **"And David's sons were priests"** <note>original Hebrew: ובני דוד כהנים היו; see 2 Samuel 8:18</note>. I deliberately translate this as *priests*, namely, according to the mind of the ancient Rabbis, before whom Aben Esra and all more recent scholars ought to rise with reverence. Kimchi <note>David Kimchi (1160–1235), also known as Radak, a medieval biblical commentator</note> cites these [ancient rabbis] regarding this passage: **"And in the words of our Rabbis of blessed memory: They were disciples of the wise [scholars]. ---

> (*) Nor did any other of the Rabbis [agree]. Buxtorf’s <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a famous Christian Hebraist</note> opinion in his lexicon is that in the two cited passages, the word should be taken to mean "Governor" etc. Evidently, he incorrectly understood the word **rabba** <note>Aramaic: רבא, meaning great or chief</note> from the Targum <note>ancient Aramaic translations/paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible</note>, to which he appeals. For **rabba** signifies *great* or *master*, a title that fits priests beautifully, as they were the greatest in the province; for the kings themselves often performed sacred rites, whether for political reasons or so that splendor might be added to the worship.

<column-break/>

<page-num>31</page-num>

**"Priests. Just as a priest takes his portion at the head [first], so too a disciple of the wise [scholar] takes his portion at the head, etc."** <note>original Hebrew: כהנים . מה כהן נוטל חלק בראש . אף תלמיד חכם נוטל חלק בראש וכולי</note> *In the words of our masters it is held that they (the sons of David) were learned men. As to why the text calls them priests, the reason is: they were like priests; for just as priests receive the firstfruits (**terumah** <note>original Hebrew: תרומה; a "heave offering" or contribution given to priests</note>, about which more shortly), so also the learned are the first to receive.* From this it appears clearly enough that the ancient Hebrews judged that the word **cohen** <note>Hebrew for priest</note> should never be understood as anything other than *priest*. If you wish to defend their position, it must be said: one **k** <note>The Hebrew letter Kaph (כ), which means "as" or "like" when used as a prefix</note> was absorbed through elision <note>the dropping of a sound or letter</note>, as anyone who has examined and understood the original text even slightly will not rarely have discovered. And now the meaning emerges: *the sons of David were like priests*. Nor (to confess the truth of the matter) is this view entirely to be despised

The explanation of these things can be defended from the Book of Chronicles: (1) there, almost all the words are repeated, and instead of ***David’s sons were priests*** <term>cohanim: the Hebrew word for priests</term>, there is substituted <note>original Hebrew: ראשונים, *rishonim*</note> **"and the sons of David were the first (at the King's side)."** Those scholars claim that this prerogative of status—being at the King's side—is what was indicated by the word *cohanim* <note>original Hebrew: כהנים</note>. I have gradually wandered off into another matter; I recall myself. <meta>This page continues the author's argument against the idea that King David held a literal priestly office. Having previously discussed the linguistic meaning of "priest" (cohen), the author now examines the legal right to receive tithes, comparing David's status to that of the biblical Melchizedek and the tribe of Levi.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

It is more certain that he attributes the right of Tithes to David, so that at least in these words—***according to the order of Melchizedek*** <note>original Latin: "secundum ordinem Malchi-Tzedek"</note>—he might instill some meaning. "**David will receive**" (he says in the place cited above) "**tithes, just as Melchizedek received them from Abraham**"; however, Melchizedek received them by right of the Priesthood, as witnessed by Jarchi <note>Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval Jewish commentator</note> and all the commentaries. Now, since it has been proven that David could not be a priest, it follows that the right to tithes is falsely attributed to him—a right that belonged formerly to the title of the priesthood, and later to the Levite. Of the tithes which were later enjoined upon the Israelites in the law, none of these applied to David at all. For we find only two kinds: <note>original Hebrew: מעשר ראשון, *Ma'aser Rishon*</note> **First Tithes** (1) intended for the Levites as an inheritance, because they [did not have] land from

(1) Numbers 18:26.

from which they might sow crops. The second kind is prescribed in Deuteronomy (1), intended for the use of the needy and for sacrifices. None of these belonged to the king. Unless, perhaps, you should think that he shared in a portion of the enemy's spoils and plunder. In the book of Numbers (2) there is a law concerning spoils; specifically regarding a portion of them to be set aside in verse 28. But it is far from the truth that the leaders and princes had any special share of the things brought back from Midian <note>A biblical people often in conflict with the Israelites</note>; rather, verse 27 commands that the plunder be divided equally. Indeed, it is commanded that the setting aside be done only for the LORD; a five-hundredth part was to be handed over to Eleazar the priest <note>The son of Aaron and his successor as High Priest</note> **"as an offering to the Lord"** <note>original Latin: "in primitias Domini"</note> (as it says there); and the same law applied to the <note>original Hebrew: תרומה, *terumah*</note> **heave offering**, or fiftieth part, which from the share of the sons— <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the discussion of the division of spoils among the "sons of Israel."</note>

—and since the priesthood was established by blood lineage itself, why would he <note>Referring to the prophet Isaiah in Isaiah 66:21</note> say, *“and even from them?”* <note>original Latin: "et etiam ex illis?"</note> Did he have any others besides those? Furthermore, offspring born from such a marriage are named in the <term>Talmud<gloss>the central text of Rabbinic Judaism containing legal and theological discussions</gloss></term> as <term>chalalim<gloss>Hebrew: חללים</gloss></term>, meaning *profaned*, and are expressly called <term>pesulim lakehunah<gloss>Hebrew: פסולים לכהונה</gloss></term>, meaning *unfit for the priesthood*. For if, as we have argued thus far, the MESSIAH will be a **priest**, and a priest not of the old law but of the new; then finally I ask: what is the meaning of the word <term>leolam<gloss>Hebrew: לעולם</gloss></term>? He will say: *“forever”* <note>original Latin: "in æternum"</note>. I do not disagree. I only ask one thing: why, contrary to every standard of word placement and setting aside the descriptive words, is it placed in the middle, immediately after the word *priest* <note>Hebrew: כהן (*cohen*)</note>? The natural order would have demanded: *priest according to the order of Melchizedek forever.* <note>The author is pointing out that in Psalm 110:4, the phrase "forever" appears immediately after "priest" rather than at the end of the sentence.</note> Far be it from me to nitpick the word order; I simply press the Hebrews, from whom I learned: *There is not a single letter in the Torah upon which there do not hang mountains of laws.* <note>original Hebrew: אין לך אות שבתורה שאינו תלויה בה הררים של הלכות — a famous Rabbinic principle emphasizing that every detail of the scripture is significant.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the Messiah's priesthood requires a change in the Mosaic Law, as traditional law excluded those of "profane" lineage. He further examines the specific phrasing of Psalm 110 to prove the Messiah initiates a permanent, new priestly order.</summary>
<keywords>Priesthood, Leviticus, Ezra, Talmud, Messiah, Melchizedek, Law of Moses, Hebrew grammar, New Law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological argument regarding the nature of the Messiah’s priesthood. Building on the previous discussion of legal requirements, the author now analyzes the specific phrasing of the Psalms to prove that the Messiah holds an eternal, heavenly office rather than a temporary, earthly one.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<meta>catchword: bus</meta>

*“There is not a stroke in the scripture from which mountains of meanings do not hang”*; <note>original Latin: *non est apex in scriptura, unde non pendeant montes sententiarum*. This is a Latin rendering of a famous Rabbinic maxim from the Talmud (Menachot 29b) regarding the "tittle" or small marks on Hebrew letters.</note> therefore, the Psalm does not speak wrongly by a changed order of words, as it might seem; rather, the order ought to have been exactly as it is, so that it might rightly express what it wished to be understood. The term <term>cohen<gloss>Hebrew: כהן, meaning "priest"</gloss></term> follows as a description and, so to speak, in the place of a quality, the term <term>leolam<gloss>Hebrew: לעולם, meaning "forever" or "unto eternity"</gloss></term>. If this is to be translated exactly as it lies, it is in the dative case: *to eternity*; but if instead we embrace it as a genitive—for the genitive case using the letter <term>Lamed<gloss>the Hebrew prefix ל- (*le-*) which usually means "to" or "for," but can indicate possession</gloss></term> is very common among the Hebrews, as no Grammarian would deny (*)—then it becomes *of eternity*. In that case, it was clearly appropriate and necessary for it to be placed thus, with this sense: *priest of eternity*. The authority of the Chaldean <note>Referring to the Targum, the ancient Aramaic translations and paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible</note> supports this (lest it seem we are bringing this forward only from our own resources

—lest the danger of controversies <note>original: *lis*</note> loom over us), for he translates it as *of the world to come* <note>Aramaic: לעלמא דאתי (*le’alma de’atei*)</note>. <meta>The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing Jesus’s questioning of the legal experts regarding the Messiah being the son of David. This page continues the argument that the title "Lord" (Ha-Adon) applied to the Messiah implies his divinity.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<meta>catchword: tatem,</meta>

-d <note>Continuing from the previous page: "how could he here hail him as his Lord?"</note>? That it must be taken as admitted. For this indeed is the rule of a just Interpretation: that while all other things are observed, that also should not be neglected, *about which the discourse is held*. Just as it is the custom to call a cobbler <note>original Latin: *cerdonem*</note>, or any other of the common people, "Lord," no less than a Royal governor <note>original Latin: *satrapam*</note> or the Archon <note>term: Archon — a chief magistrate or ruler in a city-state</note> of a city; yet we do not mean that we have bestowed equal honor on both by that name. Thus **The Lord** <note>original Hebrew: האדון *Ha-Adon*</note> is used both of GOD and, in this passage, of the MESSIAH; but the distinction is understood of itself. Moreover, so that they might be less confused, elsewhere—as we ourselves confess—everywhere **Jehovah** <note>original Hebrew: יהוה</note> is a companion <note>i.e., the words "The Lord" and "Jehovah" appear together</note>, this single place excepted, the reason for which is at hand: because in other places GOD has made Himself the subject of the speech; here it is the MESSIAH, who becomes man, and to whom therefore the name **Jehovah** <note>original Hebrew: יהוה</note> cannot apply. I resolve <note>original Latin: *diluo*, literally "wash away" or "dissolve"</note> this difficulty, however great it may be,

<page-num>59</page-num>

if I show that the name of the MESSIAH, **Jehovah** <note>original Hebrew: יהוה</note>, can be added even without the Hebrews objecting. Why could it not be, when the Talmud (1) <note>term: Talmud — the central text of Rabbinic Judaism, consisting of the Mishnah and the Gemara.</note>, which they are ashamed to contradict, discusses it thus: "Three are called by the name of the Holy One, blessed be He <note>original: *Hakadosh Baruch Hu*, a common Hebrew way of referring to God</note>. And these are: the Righteous, the Messiah, and Jerusalem. The Righteous, as it is said: *Everyone who is called by my name, and for my glory I have created him, I have formed him, indeed I have made him* <note>Isaiah 43:7</note>. The Messiah, as it is written: *And this is his name by which he shall be called: Jehovah our Righteousness* <note>Jeremiah 23:6</note>. Jerusalem, as it is written: *And the name of the city from that day shall be: Jehovah is there* <note>Ezekiel 48:35</note>. Do not read *her name* [shemah], but rather *there* [shammah]." Rabbi Samuel son of Nachman said, in the name of Rabbi Jochanan: "Three were called by the name of Jehovah: the Righteous, the MESSIAH, and Jerusalem, etc." Furthermore and more explicitly (2): "What is the name of the Messiah? Rabbi Abba said: **Jehovah** is his name."

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V.</header>

### Concerning the Names and Characteristics of the Messiah, and His Divinity Deduced from Them.

Once I used to wonder at, but now I revere the **Angel**, whom the sacred scriptures describe with such great distinction from all others—lifting him as far above the dignity of the rest as he is different from them. Consider his name and his works; notice the worship <note>original Latin: *cultum*—here referring to religious devotion or veneration</note> shown to him by men of a more holy life. All these things are wonders, and they do not so much hide the **mystery** <note>original Greek: μυσήριον *mysterion* — a hidden divine truth</note> as they reveal it. He first reveals himself to Jacob as he returns from Laban (1). Here, a singular point occurs, though it has been noticed by others: that Jacob extorted a blessing from him as if by force, and obtained it. Jacob—what a great man he was!—obtaining a blessing from the Ange- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, to be continued as "Angel" on the following page.</note>

...went before them in the **pillar of cloud**; this Angel is **Jehovah** <note>original Hebrew: יהוה *Jehovah*</note>.

Furthermore, he is the **Angel of the Covenant** <note>original Hebrew: מלאך הברית *Malakh Ha-Berit*</note>. By the word "Covenant," I understand the Law: for the Law is constantly called a "covenant" <note>original Hebrew: ברית *berith*</note> in Scripture (1); just as the Ark, in which the tablets of the Ten Commandments are kept, is called the **Ark of the Covenant** <note>original Hebrew: ארון הברית *Aron Ha-Berit*</note>. Moreover, Meir Gabbai <note>Meir ben Ezekiel ibn Gabbai (1480–1540), an influential Sephardic Kabbalist</note> acknowledges this, following the thought of Jochanan <note>likely referring to a traditional teaching attributed to the Talmudic sage Rabbi Yochanan</note>, because the Covenant—the Law—was given through him. From this it follows (again according to these same authors) that he is also **God** <note>original Hebrew: אלהים *Elohim*</note>, a point to which the Rabbi appeals.

He is the **Presence** <note>original Hebrew: פנים *Panim*, literally "Faces"</note> or the **Angel of the Presence** <note>original Hebrew: מלאך הפנים *Malakh Ha-Panim*</note>. But what is this "presence"? We find Moses praying (2): "**If your presence does not go [with us]**" <note>original Hebrew: אם אין פניך הלכים *Im ein panekha holekhim* from Exodus 33:15</note>.

<page-num>73</page-num>
<sig>E 5</sig>

St. Jerome <note>The 4th-century translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> rendered this: *if you yourself [do not go]*, etc. This is correct: for the Presence of God *is* God. For this reason, the translator Onkelos <note>The author of the *Targum Onkelos*, the primary Aramaic translation of the Torah used in Jewish liturgy</note> also translated it as: "**if your Majesty does not go with us**" <note>original Aramaic: הלא במדך שכינתך עמנא *Hala bimadakh Shekhinatakh amana*</note>. Therefore, he is the **Shekhinah** <note>original Hebrew: שכינה *Shekhinah*; a term used in Jewish tradition to describe the dwelling or settling of the divine presence</note> and **Jah** <note>original Hebrew: יה *Jah*</note>: the **Lord and Ruler**, based on the words of Nachmanides <note>also known as Ramban, a leading medieval Jewish scholar</note> cited at the end. Although these individual points are contained within the recorded meditations of the Rabbis in such a way that no Jew could evade them, I nevertheless wish to give them further strength and authority from Isaiah (1): "**I will mention the lovingkindnesses of Jehovah**," etc. "**And he was their Savior. And the Angel of his presence saved them in his love and in his pity**" <note>original Hebrew: יהוה חסדי אזכיר וגו׳ ויהי להם למושיע ומלאך פניו הושיעם באהבתו ובחמלתו from Isaiah 63:7–9</note>.

(1) Isaiah, chapter 63, verse 7 and following.
(o) I gave my word in my *Grammatical Institutions* [a previous work by the author] that I would provide the reason for the *Dagesh lene* <note>a grammatical dot in a Hebrew letter indicating a "harder" pronunciation</note> in the word "lovingkindnesses" <note>original Hebrew: חסדי *chasdei*</note>. Here I resolve it. It is found three times with this mark, and in every instance, the discourse concerns the Messiah. Therefore, in these places, it should be regarded as a Masoretic mark <note>The Masoretes were the scholars who standardized the Hebrew Bible's pronunciation and punctuation; the author implies the grammar here points specifically to Christ</note>.

<meta>catchword: uti-</meta>

**Jehovah**, are our Father and our **Redeemer**, etc. The Prophet enumerates the benefits provided to the Israelites by Jehovah: and he attributes their operation to this **Angel of the Presence**; he calls him the **Savior**, he calls him the **Redeemer**. After they had provoked this Angel to anger and he had **fought against them**, the Prophet again longingly seeks him, recounting those things which he had provided for them in the days of Moses: among which is also that phrase <note>original Hebrew: מוליך לימין משה *molekh l'yamin Mosheh*</note> **leading Moses by his right hand**. This action is elsewhere (1) attributed to GOD, whom the Rabbi <note>referring to the Rabbinic authorities cited on previous pages, like Meir Gabbai</note> admitted was the Angel of the Presence. Having reviewed these things, the Prophet finally speaks thus: *You are our Father: Abraham has not known us: Israel has not recognized us; You, Jehovah, are our Father and our Redeemer.* What then? Shall we say the Prophet has forgotten Isaac? <meta>The previous page established that the Angel of the Lord, often identified as Metatron, bears the divine name "Jehovah" because God's "name is in him." <page-num>88</page-num>
<meta>catchword: twice,</meta>

he says (1): **"The name Jehovah is not spoken in any respect, except concerning the First Cause."** <note>original Hebrew: *Shem Jehovah lo ne'emar be-shum tzad, ki im al ha-sibbah ha-rishonah*. The "First Cause" is a philosophical term for God as the ultimate origin of all things.</note> *The name Jehovah cannot be said except of the first creator.* In agreement with this are the opinions of Rabbi Meir Gabbai (2) and Rabbi Joseph Albo (3), and finally of the author of the *Beginning of Wisdom* (4) <note>original Hebrew: *Reshit Chochmah*, a 16th-century kabbalistic work by Elijah de Vidas</note>. ---

> concerning its grammatical qualities, which are generally familiar to proper names: I. It does not admit a plural form. II. It rejects the ה <note>The Hebrew letter 'He', used as a definite article "the"</note> of identification. III. It is not turned into a "construct state" <note>A Hebrew grammatical form showing possession</note>: instead of IV, in our grammar we substitute that the name יהוה *Jehovah* can never be joined with a pronoun. Even if we are permitted to take these same properties as an argument—the same ones we find in שדי *Shaddai* <term>Almighty</term>—and demonstrate from there that it is also a proper name: nevertheless, Jarchi <note>Rashi, the famous 11th-century commentator</note> attributes that name as a proper name to this Angel.

twice over, that not one person denies that the name יהוה *Jehovah* <note>The author uses the Greek article "τὸ" before the Hebrew name</note> belongs to God alone, as an innate and powerful designation: by which He first deigned to manifest His unoriginated substance (so to speak) to Moses, having hidden it even from the Patriarchs. You understand now why Solomon, filled with wonder, asks: <note>original Hebrew: מי הקים כל אפסי ארץ שמו ומה שם בנו כי תדע</note> ***"Who has established all the ends of the earth? What is his name, and what is his son's name, if you know?"*** <note>Proverbs 30:4</note> Rightly did Raymond Martini <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar known for using Hebrew texts to argue for Christian doctrines</note> and others following him adapt this passage to assert the personhood of the Son within the Deity; but we draw something else from it as well. To be sure, no one knew the name of the one *establishing all the ends of the earth*, but now that has been revealed to Moses and to everyone. In what, therefore, does Solomon's *"If you know"* consist? In this: that both He and His Son are marked by an equal name—both **Jehovah** <term>The four-letter name of God, often considered too sacred to pronounce</term> and **Shaddai** <term>A title for God usually translated as 'Almighty'</term>. This fact is to be numbered among the secrets hidden from most by a veil of ignorance, and for that reason, it could deservedly be proposed in the form of a question.

## CHAPTER V.

### *On the works of the Messiah: His victory against the demon, his satisfaction for the original sin, and the changing of the law: each point drawn again from the confessions of the Rabbis.*

<margin>(1) Numbers 21:14.</margin>
Whenever I touch upon the phrase <note>original Hebrew: על כן יאמר בספר מלחמות יהוה וגו'</note> **"Therefore it will be said in the Book of the Wars of Jehovah, etc."** (1), I reject the opinion of the many scholars—among whom is Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish rabbi and biblical commentator</note>—who believe that the *Book of the Wars of Jehovah* has perished, just like the *Prophecies of Nathan*, the *Prophecies of Iddo*, and the *Chronicles of the Kings of Israel*. With all due respect to the authority of those who think otherwise, the other books we lack are not in any way comparable to this one. Would the Hebrews not have applied all their diligence to preserving this specific book?

<meta>catchword: in-</meta>

---

<margin>(1) Exodus 17:14. (2) Deuteronomy 25:19. (3) In *Zeror Hamor*, page 120.</margin>
-tended to it? Furthermore, why is this book mentioned only once, while the others are mentioned so many times? Indeed, the book is present, but it is one and the same as the Law <note>The Torah</note>; mention of it is made in Exodus (1): <note>original Hebrew: כתוב זאת זכרון בספר ושים באזני יהושע כי מחה אמחה את זכר עמלק</note> **"Write this for a memorial in a book and deliver it into the ears of Joshua; for I will utterly blot out the memory of Amalek."** Both the Paraphrase of Jonathan and the Jerusalem Paraphrase <note>Ancient Aramaic translations and explanations of the Bible, known as Targums</note> translate this as "in the Law" <note>original Aramaic: אורייתא</note>. This very same point is repeated in Deuteronomy (2).

Others among the Rabbis have taken a different path from the opinions mentioned above. Among their writings, I find the following regarding the verse quoted earlier (3): <note>original Hebrew: על דרך הרמז נראה שייעד בכאן הגאלה העתידה ועל כן אמר . על כן יאמר לזמן העתיד . בספר מלחמות יהוה . מהו מלחמות יהוה היא מלחמת</note> **"By way of an allusion, it appears that he designated the future redemption here, and therefore he said: 'Therefore it will be said'—referring to the future time—'in the Book of the Wars of Jehovah.' **He shall restore**, therefore, as the Hebrew himself asserts, whatever was corrupted through Adam; the **MESSIAH** will purge the filth of the serpent. Sin is not a "passive injury" (for the Majesty of GOD stands far higher and beyond such a thing), but it is *active*, or viewed from the side of the one committing the offense. The Old Law prescribes the manner of expiating this (for there is no "satisfaction" or repayment for sin unless DIVINE MERCY chooses to accept it): it requires that those guilty of sin should offer a sacrifice, or a victim, for that sin. <meta>The author continues the argument that the Messiah’s sacrifice was necessary to reconcile humanity with God. He transitions from the requirement for an innocent priest to the requirement for a perfect sacrifice, using Rabbinic literature to argue that animals are inherently "blemished" and therefore insufficient to atone for the original sin of Adam and Eve.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>❦ ⁎ ⁎ ❦</header>

...from the first man <note>The text likely refers to Adam</note>, I say, forever? No man. Therefore, the coming of the **MESSIAH OF GOD** was entirely necessary for us, so that through Him, as an innocent one, we might be reconciled to **GOD**. For this reason—since every other priest was rendered incapable of offering the victim of atonement because of that hereditary stain—He also had to be a **priest** <term>Cohen</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Kohen*</note> to offer the sacrifice. This sacrifice, however, had to be a bloody one, according to the scripture (1): **For it is the blood that makes atonement for the soul** <note>original Hebrew: *ki ha-dam hu ba-nefesh yekhapper*; Leviticus 17:11</note>. To us Christians, it is a plain matter that the blood of goats and the fat of rams could not be an equal atonement for the offense; therefore, the altogether infinite price of the **MOST DIVINE BLOOD** was required to appease the **FATHER'S** anger against the human race—

---

(1) Leviticus 17:11.

<column-break/>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>❦ ⁎ ⁎ ❦</header>

—race. Indeed, for the Jews, this same conclusion flows from the very doctrine of the Rabbis: that a victim chosen from animals could not be set apart to pay for this original guilt. For they assert (1): **She (Eve) gave both the domestic and wild animals, as well as the birds, the forbidden fruit to eat; and they all obeyed her, etc.** <note>original Hebrew: *ha-akhilah et ha-behemah ve-et ha-ḥayyah ve-et ha-ofot ha-kol sham‘u lah*. The author is quoting the Midrash Rabbah on Genesis, a collection of ancient Jewish interpretations of the Bible.</note> From this it follows that all animals also contracted the stain of sin themselves; and they are not wrongly called **blemished** <term>ba‘ale mumin</term> <note>original Hebrew: *ba‘alei mumim*, literally "possessors of defects"</note>. Everyone knows that creatures of this kind are unfit for the service of the altar. You may ask: was it permitted for those animals to be sacrificed for sin—or more accurately, for the sinful human? Why not? Certainly, when they desired to be purified from other individual sins; but it is otherwise when one seeks to be purified from— <note>The catchword "ab" suggests the sentence continues "from original guilt" on the following page.</note>

---

(1) Midrash Rabbah on Genesis.

<summary>The author argues that human priests and animal sacrifices are insufficient to atone for original sin because all of creation was "tainted" when Eve fed the forbidden fruit to the animals. Consequently, only a divine, innocent Messiah acting as a priest can offer a truly "unblemished" sacrifice.</summary>

...from the original stain <note>Latin: *originali labe*, referring to original sin</note>. Isaiah, where he prophesies about the **MESSIAH**, argues thus (1): **יהוה חפץ דכאו החלי . אם תשים אשם נפשו . יראה זרע יאריך ימים וחפץ יהוה בידו יצליח** <note>Hebrew: "Yet it pleased the Lord to bruise him; he hath put him to grief: when his soul shall make an offering for sin, he shall see his seed, he shall prolong his days, and the pleasure of the Lord shall prosper in his hand." —the words of the Jonathan Paraphrase <term>Targum Jonathan</term> <note>An ancient Aramaic translation and interpretation of the Prophets</note> testify, which it adds at the end of this text: **יחזון במלכות משיחהון וכו'** <note>Aramaic: "They shall look upon the kingdom of their Messiah, etc."</note> *they shall see the reign of the MESSIAH, etc.* Nor does Ibn Ezra <note>A famous 12th-century Jewish scholar</note> deny it, whose exposition on the cited passage is: **והנה ידבר על הדור וכו'** and **בעת קץ ביאת המשיח** <note>Hebrew: "And behold, he speaks concerning the generation... at the time of the end, the coming of the Messiah."</note> *and behold, he speaks of the generation, etc. when the end comes: namely, the time of the coming of the MESSIAH.* This being established, let us be more attentive to the words of the text: **אם תשים אשם נפשו** <note>Hebrew: *im tashim asham nafsho*</note> *if he shall lay down his soul (as an offering) for sin*, so says **St. Jerome** <note>The 4th-century translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible</note>: and rightly so, as far as the meaning is concerned. However, let us be permitted to call upon grammatical analysis for support. **תשים** <note>The Hebrew verb *tashim*</note> is from the root **שום** <note>The root *sh-w-m*, to put or place</note>, it is in the *Hiphil* <term>Hiphil</term> <note>The causative form of a Hebrew verb</note> form, future tense, third person: therefore it should be translated as *he shall cause to be placed* (that is, *he shall place*).

<page-num>126</page-num>

pertains); but **ת** <note>The Hebrew letter Tav, used here as a feminine grammatical marker</note> is a feminine sign; therefore the subject of the verb is feminine. There is no other feminine noun present except **נפש** <note>Hebrew: *Nephesh*, meaning soul, life, or being</note>; therefore, a literal translation <note>Greek: *κατά πόδα* (kata poda), meaning "following the feet" or word-for-word</note> would be: *if his soul makes (itself) an offering for sin.* According to our view, Kimchi <note>Rabbi David Kimhi (c. 1160–1235), a preeminent medieval Jewish scholar and grammarian</note> explains it this way: **ואם שמה, אם-נפשו עצמה במקום אשם** <note>Hebrew: "And if it places—if his soul itself [stands] in the place of the guilt offering"</note> *if the soul itself should stand in the place of the offering for sin.* Therefore, the soul of the **MESSIAH** will be for sin; He Himself will be the offering, and an offering free from the Adamic stain <note>Latin: *labis Adamaticæ*, referring to the inherited "original sin" of Adam</note>, since He is **GOD**. The single word **covenant** <note>Hebrew: ברית (*Berit*)</note> builds a transition toward the changing of the old law and the substitution of a new law to be established by the **MESSIAH**. For where **GOD** had promised the reunion of the tribes through the **MESSIAH**, He adds: <note>Hebrew: Ezekiel 37:26, וכרתי להם ברית שלום (*Vekarati lahem berit shalom*)</note> *and I will strike for them a covenant of peace*. Why does He say, "I will strike"? <note>The author is playing on the literal meaning of the Latin *percutiam* and the Hebrew *karat*, which both mean "to cut" or "to strike" a covenant, often referring to the sacrifice that sealed the deal.</note> He had already made this [covenant] with Abraham and his offspring (1), and with Moses and the people (2), even when the **Tabernacle of the Covenant** (3) was erected; He also reminds them of the covenant struck at Horeb (4). Why, then, was it necessary for the covenant to be renewed? It was necessary for it to be struck [anew] entirely; for the new covenant is the *covenant of peace*. A new covenant, I say, shall be sanctioned through the **MESSIAH**, whom countless passages of Scripture prove will be the **Lawgiver** <note>original Greek: νομοθέτην (*nomotheten*)</note>. ...according to the prophecy of Jeremiah; I do not infer it myself: let the Hebrews infer, if they can, anything other than to conclude the abolition of their laws.

Finally, lest the Jews complain of bias, we even grant the Rabbis freedom of opinion regarding the duration of the laws (1): <note>original Hebrew: כמו שהרופא יתן הנהגה אל החולה עד זמן משוער אצלו . שלא יגלהו אל החולה עד זמן משוער אצלו . וכשיגיע הזמן ההוא שנתחזק החולה כבר מחליו . ישנה הרופא הנהגתו . ויתיר מה שאסר . ויאסור מה שהתיר . ואין לחולה להיפלא מזה . כך כשנתן השם יתברך התורה ידע שהנהגה ההיא הספיק עד הזמן ששיערה חכמתו שיספוק להכין המקבלים ולתקן טבעם אל שיקבלו ההנהגה . אף על פי</note> *“Just as a physician might give a regimen to a sick person for a time estimated by him, which he does not reveal to the patient until that estimated time; and when that time arrives, and the patient has already grown strong from his illness, the physician changes his regimen, permitting what he had forbidden and forbidding what he had permitted—and the patient should not wonder at this—so too, when **God (blessed be He)** gave the Torah, He knew that this regimen would suffice until the time His wisdom estimated it would be enough to prepare those receiving it and to refine their nature to receive [a new] regimen. Even though...”*

<margin>(1) Rabbi Joseph Albo in the book *Ikkarim* <term>Ikkarim: "The Book of Principles," a 15th-century work of Jewish philosophy</term>, Book 3, Chapter 13.</margin>

<meta>catchword: though</meta>

<column-break/>

<header>❦ * * ❦</header>

<note>original Hebrew: פי שלא גילהו לאדם . וכשיגיע הזמן יצוה בהנהגה השנית וכולי :</note> *“...He did not reveal it to man; and when the time arrives, He will command the second regimen, etc.”* Just as a physician is accustomed to prescribe a rule for a sick person, to last for a time known to the physician, but which he will not reveal to the one lying ill; but when that hour comes in which the patient is freed from his infirmity, the physician will also change the former rule: he will allow the patient what he had previously forbidden, and forbid what he had previously allowed. Nor does this seem strange to the patient. So also, when **God (Blessed be He)** gave the Law, it was known to Him that this rule was beneficial until the time which His wisdom intended for the preparation and restoration of human nature; although He revealed this to no one, when the time arrives, He will prescribe a second rule. Therefore, they themselves do not deny that there will be another law following the Mosaic one, when the time...

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page serves as the table of contents and introduction for Book II. It transitions from the theological arguments of Book I—which focused on the nature of the Messiah and the "new law"—into the historical and chronological evidence for the Messiah’s arrival.</meta>

# OUTLINE.

**Chapter I.** The arrival of the Messiah must have occurred while Jerusalem and the Temple were standing: therefore, before the destruction of both.
**Chapter II.** The month and the day of redemption, which coincide with the fall of Adam.
**Chapter III.** The reconciliation of the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John</note>, who seem to be in conflict regarding the hour of CHRIST'S death; however, they only appear to be so.
**Chapter IV.** Christ suffered on that day, the evening of which was the beginning of the Passover for the Jews. Nevertheless, He rightly and lawfully ate the Passover with His disciples on the previous day. A mystical rite, still practiced among the Jews today, is added.
**Chapter V.** The reason for the prohibition of leavened bread, based on an analysis of the texts of Exodus Chapter 12, verses 34 and 35.

Someone will say that we are merely repeating what has already been done; that the arguments treated in the schools and everywhere else are already convincing—those drawn from the verse *the scepter shall not be taken away from Judah* etc. (1) <note>original: "non auferetur sceptrum de juda"; a reference to the prophecy that a ruler would always remain in the tribe of Judah until the Messiah comes.</note> and from the "weeks of Daniel" <note>A prophecy in Daniel 9 used by Christians to calculate the exact time of Christ's arrival.</note>. ...interpreted, even for one who omits circumcision: to such a person, if he performs penance, the Day of Atonement (1) grants forgiveness. I know well enough what might be objected; namely, that the MESSIAH cannot come on this day, which is called the <note>original Hebrew: שבת שבתון</note> *Sabbath of Sabbaths*: he cannot come then, one might say, any more than on any other Sabbath, since it is not permitted to travel more than two thousand paces <note>A "Sabbath day's journey," the maximum distance a Jew is traditionally permitted to walk on the holy day</note>. This is quite right for that specific day. But why does he delay during the next four days which lie between the *Day of Atonement* and the *Feast of Tabernacles*? For on these days too the Jews are pure, because God, during their passing, imputes no sin to the Hebrews. So says the Midrash (2): <note>original Hebrew: ולקחתם לכם ביום הראשון. וכי ראשון הוא והלא ט"ו בו אלא ראשון הוא</note> *And you shall take for yourselves on the first day. Is it the first? ### ❦ ❦ * * * ❦ ❦ ###

...it is the first for the reckoning of sins, etc. *You shall receive it on the first day, etc. Is this the first* (of the month)? (*Is it not rather the fifteenth?*) But *it is the first day on which* GOD *imputes sins (to the Jews); for four days between the Day of Atonement and the Feast of Tabernacles, sins are not imputed.* If, therefore, sins are not imputed, they are pious; if they are pious, why does he not come? While I press the Jew with this kind of question, I gain something that contributes greatly to my purpose: namely, a distrust toward his own people. He sees, and cannot deny, that this word *today*—though it sounds promising—is in fact empty; and as if caught in a trap, he is held by shame to listen to what I have to say. Furthermore, I do not proceed in such a way as to suddenly rush to the goal. By leading him gradually and as if through winding turns and the bends of the paths, I reach the point to which I...

the end, and it will not lie? *Rabbi Samuel the son of Nachman said: Rabbi Jonathan said: Let the bones of those who calculate the end of time* <note>original Latin: "intumescant offa eorum, qui inquirunt finem temporis"</note> *(the arrival of the MESSIAH) burst, because they will say, when the time has passed: he will come no more. Hope for him: for it is said; if he makes a delay, wait for him, etc.* <note>The author is quoting a famous Rabbinic warning from the Talmud (Sanhedrin 97b) against trying to predict the exact date of the Messiah's coming, emphasizing that faith must persist even when expectations are frustrated.</note> Therefore, the preceding clause must also be understood as referring to the MESSIAH, as the connection of the passage proves; in it, moreover, it is commanded that he *write the vision and afterwards:* <note>original Hebrew: ובאר על הלחות למען ירוץ קורא בו</note> **explain it upon the tablets, so that he who reads it may run.** <note>Quoting Habakkuk 2:2. **and explained in the book of the law** (meaning the Law of Moses) **so that anyone reading in it might be able to understand.** For the word *tablets* <note>Greek/Hebrew: τὸ לוחות (the luchot)</note> is taken to mean the Law of Moses (which is indeed common usage), and for 그 reason we are referred back to the Law, to understand from it **that the MESSIAH, coming, will come and will not delay.**

For that reason, let us search in the Law and its five books. Since they <note>referring to Jewish commentators</note> consider it ambiguous whether the prophecy of Jacob <note>A reference to Genesis 49:10, the "Scepter of Judah" prophecy.</note> was spoken about the MESSIAH—though they cannot truly doubt it—let us hear the prophecy of Balaam: <margin>(1) Numbers chapter 24, verse 17.</margin> (1) <note>original Hebrew: דרך כוכב מיעקב וקם שבט מישראל וגו'</note> **A star shall rise out of Jacob, and a scepter shall rise out of Israel, etc.** So that the Christian may be certain that the Jews also refer this to the MESSIAH, I cite all three paraphrases <note>The Targums: Onkelos, Pseudo-Jonathan, and the Jerusalem Targum.</note> and also Rabbis from among the more recent ones.
