<header>❦ * * * ❦</header>

## CHAPTER II.

### *The Month and Day of liberation, which coincide with the fall of Adam.*

It is strange, and not easy to discern, for what reason **GOD** has hidden the time of Spiritual redemption so deeply: so that one can only extract the period through precise reasoning, the month with difficulty, and the day itself hardly at all. This is strange in itself, but you would be even more amazed when you consider this: that long before, the duration of the Egyptian servitude was signaled to Abraham, as was the period under the judges and kings, and the departure from the Babylonian captivity through the Prophets; as if those events were of greater importance or required more effort than this help shining upon the whole world? If there is room for conjecture (while respecting the inviolable sanctuary of Divine judgments), I would say: the purpose of this silence among the Prophets
<meta>purpose (finem)</meta>

---

<page-num>183</page-num>
<header>❦ * * * ❦</header>

the purpose seems to be: so that the calling of faith might contain greater merit; for if the day of salvation had been certain to everyone, they would have very easily recognized him whom they were now certainly awaiting. Conversely, through the darkness of such a great work, room was made for the faith of the Canaanite woman <note>original Latin: Chananitidis; referring to the woman in Matthew 15:22 whose daughter was healed</note>, the centurion <note>referring to the Roman soldier whose faith Jesus praised as greater than any in Israel</note>, the blind man sitting by the road, and along with many others, Peter, the Prince of the Apostles. By this faith, those people earned the immediate help and praise of **JESUS**, and Peter even earned the primacy for founding the church. Truly, those same shadows surround the Jews even today, at the time when we glory in the calling to faith in **CHRIST**. However, once the period of the coming **MESSIAH** has been fixed, one should not labor anxiously over the month and day. Let no one consider this a primary matter, nor a point of controversy; for which reason, regarding this matter
<sig>M 4</sig>
<meta>dis- (discussing)</meta>

<summary>The author explores why God chose to obscure the exact timing of Christ’s arrival compared to the clearly prophesied dates of earlier biblical events, concluding it was to test and reward the merit of faith. He references New Testament figures whose faith was proven by their recognition of Jesus despite this chronological uncertainty.</summary>
<keywords>Redemption, Prophecy, Timing of Christ, Faith, Canaanite woman, Centurion, St. Peter, Messiah, Divine Silence</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 183, which discussed how the hidden nature of the time of redemption served to test and reward the faith of early believers like Peter and the Canaanite woman.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>

->❦ * * * ❦<-

if we delay, everything should be considered a gain: and whereby an equally convenient step is built, as if by a certain link, to the later chapter.

And now, since in the final chapter of Book I we proved that the guilt contracted in Adam by all those yet to be born must be paid through the **MESSIAH**, it is fitting to assert: that this resolution of original sin <note>original Latin: "noxæ originariæ"</note> must be made in the same month and on the same day on which we deplore its first commission. Therefore, a Christian (if approaching this first) will be allowed to invert the conjecture and argue: in whatever month or day **CHRIST** washed away the primal fault, on those same days it was contracted from our first parent <note>referring to Adam as the ancestor of humanity</note>.

Regarding the Month first, we know it was <term>Nissan</term> <note>original Hebrew: ניסן; the first month of the Jewish religious calendar, occurring in spring</note>, which corresponds to our March: namely, when at the time of Passover <note>original Latin: "pafchali tempore"</note> our Passover, **CHRIST**, was sacrificed:

---

<page-num>185</page-num>

->❦ * * * ❦<-

and that the Passover of the Hebrews was established in the month of *Nissan* is known even by those who know nothing else at all. Therefore, if we take up the relationship between sin and satisfaction regarding time, much is added to the opinion which maintains—and not unskillfully—that the world was truly created then. On this subject, the disputes of philosophers are countless to the point of nausea; we would hardly wish to be entangled in them! It pleases us, therefore, to report only those assertions which prevail among the Hebrews; and even these no further than they promote our purpose.

The <term>Mishnaic doctors</term> <note>Jewish scholars of the 1st and 2nd centuries AD whose legal traditions were compiled in the Mishnah</note> are divided into two main parties under two leaders who hold differing views. The leader of the first group, which defends that the world was created in the autumn season in the month of <term>Tishrei</term> <note>the seventh month of the Jewish religious calendar, occurring in late September or October</note>, which coincides with our September, is Rabbi Eliezer: and he cites for the establishing of his opinion...

<summary>The author argues that the redemption of humanity through Christ occurred on the same day and month as Adam's original sin, identifying this crucial time as the month of Nissan. He then introduces a debate among Jewish scholars regarding whether the world was created in the spring (Nissan) or autumn (Tishrei).</summary>
<keywords>Original Sin, Redemption, Nissan, Tishrei, Passover, Chronology, Mishnaic doctors, Rabbi Eliezer, Creation of the World</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 185, where the author began discussing the differing opinions of Hebrew scholars regarding the month in which the world was created, specifically transitioning into the view that aligns with autumn.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>

->❦ ⁂ ❦<-

view is supported by the phrase "a fruit-bearing tree" <note>original Hebrew: עץ עשה פרי (*etz oseh peri*); original Latin: *lignum ferens fructum*</note> (1) <margin>(1) Genesis ch. 1 v. 11.</margin>—the harvest of which occurs in September. However, the opposing view, led by Rabbi Joshua <note>a prominent 1st-century Jewish scholar</note>, holds that the creation took place in the new spring, in the month of <term>Nissan</term> <note>the first month of the Hebrew religious calendar</note> (our March). This opinion relies on the words (2) <margin>(2) ibid.</margin> "the earth brought forth grass" <note>original Hebrew: ותוצא הארץ דשא (*va-totze ha-aretz deshe*); original Latin: *produxit terra gramen*</note>, which sprouts at the beginning of spring. Neither opinion is fully satisfactory in its argument. Each separates those things which were actually made at the same time; the grass came forth from the earth, and yet the trees were already thick with fruit. The former is a sign of spring, the latter a sign of autumn; yet, because they were created on the same days, spring and autumn could not have existed simultaneously. There is no need for anxiety over the name of the season as it was then; it is enough that Scripture instructs us: the world was formed in full perfection, with the earth covered in the greenness of her- <meta>catchword: her-</meta>

---

<page-num>187</page-num>

->❦ ⁂ ❦<-

herbs, and the branches of the trees laden with fruit. This is a matter of faith. However, as to which point in the cycle of the year, or which of the four seasons, the beginning of the world falls—calculating backward from our current passage of time—that, I say, in my opinion, Rabbi Joshua divines more correctly: namely, that it returns to spring.

We are led to this conclusion, even setting aside the Rabbi's argument, by the commandment (1) <margin>(1) Exodus 12 v. 2.</margin> "This month (the context refers to Nissan) shall be for you the beginning of months; it shall be the first of the months of the year" <note>original Hebrew: החדש הזה לכם ראש חדשים ראשון הוא לכם לחדשי השנה</note>. Thus, Nissan is the start of the year, which for this reason is called by the <term>Talmudists</term> <note>scholars of the Talmud, the central text of Rabbinic Judaism</note> "Nissan, the head of the year's months" <note>original Hebrew: ניסן ראש השנה לחדשים (*Nissan rosh ha-shanah la-chodashim*)</note>. Why then should Nissan prin- <meta>catchword: potif- (principally/chiefly)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the theological debate over whether the world was created in spring (Nissan) or autumn (Tishrei), ultimately arguing for a spring creation to align with the biblical start of the months and the redemption of Christ.</summary>

...most chiefly? <note>original Latin: *potissimum*</note> In vain? No one is so impudent as to say so. But another reason will not be easily assigned if you refuse this one: that God willed, by the established order of the months, to suggest that the machine of the universe was made in this month. I am strongly confirmed in this by the sequence religiously observed in the enumeration of the other festivals according to this order, just as when it is said: (1) <margin>(1) Deuteronomy ch. 16 v. 16.</margin> "Three times a year all your males shall appear before the face of the Lord your God, etc., namely: in the Festival of Unleavened Bread, in the Festival of Weeks, and in the Festival of Booths" <note>original Hebrew: שלש פעמים בשנה יראה כל זכורך את פני יהוה אלהיך וגו׳ . בחג המצות בחג השבעות ובחג הסוכות (*Shalosh pe'amim ba-shanah yere'eh kol zekhurekha et penei Adonai Eloheikha... be-hag ha-matzot, be-hag ha-shavu'ot, u-ve-hag ha-sukkot*)</note>; *“three times in a year shall every male appear before the face of the Lord your God, etc.”* (namely) *“in the feast of Unleavened Bread, of Pentecost, and of Tabernacles.”* <note>The author provides the Latin translation of the Hebrew verse here: *ter in anno videatur omnis masculus ante faciem Domini Dei tui &c.*</note> Clearly, the Festival of Unleavened Bread <note>Azymorum</note>, which is in the month of <term>Nissan</term> <note>the first month of the Hebrew religious calendar, falling in March/April</note>, takes the lead in this order: so that from this it is also right to conjecture: that the world took its begin- <meta>catchword: -ning</meta>

---

<page-num>189</page-num>

<image-desc>Decorative flourish ornament at the top center of the page.</image-desc>

-ning from this month: because the year must be taken from there.

Indeed, I am by no means ignorant that the beginning of the year is assigned by law to the month of <term>Tishri</term> <note>the seventh month of the religious calendar, but the first of the civil calendar, falling in September/October</note>, roughly in the middle of September; from which perhaps someone might draw an argument for autumn against us; however, we are not delayed by this. For however much it might seem so, the objection will disappear, provided one is willing to turn their mind to what is actually ordained. Clearly, that "new year" <note>Rosh Hashanah</note> prescribes no other rule for time, except that by which the seventh year of rest, which they call <term>Jovel</term> <note>The Jubilee year, occurring every 50 years</note>, is to be determined; no other times are directed according to this one. Nor could any other beginning be assigned for the cycle of the Jubilee years, as <term>Aben Esra</term> <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a prolific 12th-century Spanish-Jewish scholar</note> constructs the reason most cleverly in his Commentary (on Exodus ch. 25):

*In <term>Marchesvan</term> <note>the eighth month of the Hebrew calendar, falling in October/November</note> (which you may compare to our October), the Israelites begin to sow. If, therefore, the beginning of the (resting) year were to be reckoned from Nissan, they would not reap what they sowed in the sixth year. But neither would they be able to sow: because the year of rest would still be lasting. And if they do not dare to reap the sowing of the sixth year, they will not sow twice: yet the text says: in the seventh (only) year you shall not sow. This remarkable reasoning is pleasing, for it removes every difficulty; and the beginning of the world remains fixed in Nissan. Near the world's beginning, Adam sinned: therefore in Nissan. In this*

Beyond the brevity of the uncorrupted state <note>The state of primeval innocence before the Fall.</note>, this is also cleared of doubt: that Adam had already sinned before he had known his wife as a partner in marriage; for the sacred writer <note>Moses, traditionally considered the author of Genesis.</note> records this after the expulsion from the paradise of pleasure. From this last point, the first is born again: that their happiness was not long-lasting; for it is certain that the blessing *increase and multiply* <note>original Latin: *multiplicamini*</note> was added immediately upon their creation.

Therefore, various opinions have arisen, the catalog of which I do not intend to weave (*); rather, hold what we think. A Christian easily accepts (and ought to accept) that the token of election of old—namely, circumcision—was a <term>type</term> <note>In theology, a "type" is an event or person in the Old Testament that foreshadows something in the New Testament.</note> of the reality or baptism; St. Aurelius Augustine (1) says: *circumcision was the sacrament of that time, which prefigured the baptism of our time:* nor is there lacking the authority of the Holy Fathers who believe that through circumcision (under the condition of faith in the coming liberator, and symbolically) the original stain <note>original: *originalis macula*, referring to original sin inherited from Adam.</note> was plucked out.

opinion most widely accepted among the Hebrews: that paradise is open to no uncircumcised person. I should like to draw out from them the reason why they feel this way. <meta>This page continues the theological discussion regarding circumcision as a means of expiating "hereditary guilt" (Original Sin). <page-num>200</page-num>

...those who deny that the stain of original sin <note>original: *originariam labem*</note> would be of any consequence. I return to my point: I will summarize what I have discussed at length so far: circumcision exists (with due moderation, as a significant symbol and figure) to expiate hereditary guilt. Before I continue, I must address those who ask: "What then is the expiation for women?" I answer: we do not know; however, this does not undermine our argument. For at that time, there was not yet a universal remedy, as it was not even known to the nations; and a law only binds those to whom it is given. Just as foreign peoples who were not part of the <term>Covenant</term> <note>The *foedus* or Covenant refers to the special relationship and agreement between God and the Israelites.</note> could be saved—provided they followed the eternal laws of reason and the laws written on their hearts—so too it was for girls. God did not wish to extend the physical sign of the covenant to them, even though He later extended the benefits of the covenant itself to include them.

---

<page-num>201</page-num>

Now I proceed to investigate: why was Circumcision deferred until the eighth day? This is a weightier question, to use the language of the Rabbis: **The precept of circumcision takes precedence over the Sabbath** <note>original Hebrew: *Milah bi-zmanah dokhah Shabbat* / מילה בזמנה דוחה שבת. This is a fundamental principle in Jewish law stating that the requirement to circumcise an infant on his eighth day is so important that it overrides the usual prohibitions against "wounding" or work on the Sabbath.</note>. Why is this? They say it is because the scripture commands: **"And on the eighth day, the infant shall be circumcised"** <note>original Hebrew: *ve-bayyom ha-shemini yimmol* / וביום השמיני ימול; see Leviticus 12:3.</note>. But under Rabbinic law, is it not forbidden to inflict a wound on the Sabbath? Nevertheless, if the eighth day falls on a Sabbath, the infant must be circumcised—provided the infant has lived healthily for those eight days. However, if an illness prevented the circumcision on the actual eighth day after birth, it is then no longer permitted to perform the ritual on a subsequent Sabbath. From this, I rightly conclude that the eighth day is of the highest importance, such that it does not give way even to the supreme command of the Sabbath. Therefore, it is no idle question to ask: why is it commanded...

<summary>The author argues that circumcision serves to expiate original sin and explains that women were saved through natural law. He then explores the legal priority of the "eighth day" in Jewish law, noting that the ritual must be performed even on the Sabbath if it is the correct day.</summary>

...the eighth day? <note>original: *pue* (likely *quare*)</note> Let us hear the one we cited just a moment ago as he resolves this question. (1) **I say that all this stems from the original sin, as it is written: *and she shall be cleansed from the fountain of her blood* <note>original Hebrew: וטהרה ממקור דמיה; see Leviticus 12:7</note>. For the first sin of Eve is a fountain opened for impure blood, etc. Just as we find in the Tractate *Eruvin*, in the chapter *He who carries out phylacteries* <note>original Hebrew: המוציא תפילין / *Ha-Motzi Tefillin*</note>: *I will greatly multiply your sorrow* <note>original Hebrew: הרבה ארבה עצבונך; see Genesis 3:16</note>. Indeed,

->*What I have just proposed is not a*<-
->*formal opinion* (1)<-

nevertheless, it is a conjecture that should be greatly embraced by a Christian, from which the following arises: Adam was created on the sixth day; if he sinned on the eighth day from his creation, this occurred on the Sabbath, which is the 14th day of the month. Add the month, which is Nisan <note>The first month of the Hebrew calendar, occurring in Spring</note>, and behold the day of Passover <note>original: *paschatis*</note>: the very day on which we know Christ suffered death for our salvation.

# CHAPTER III.

### *The Reconciliation of the Evangelists, who seem to be in conflict regarding the hour of CHRIST’S death: but only seem so.*

Among those things by which the authority of the Holy Scriptures is established, not the least is this: that in such a great variety of writers, subjects, and styles, they are always found to be in harmony with one another. Indeed, it is the peculiar fate of spurious writings <term>pseudographorum</term> that they overthrow themselves through the conflict of their own passages; I might say that, like venomous creatures (if what is said is to be believed), they carry their own <term>antidote</term> <note>original Greek: ἀντίδοτον</note> with them. Rabbi Isaac, who was ever the most biting of Hebrew writers, in his book entitled *The Fortification of Faith* <note>original Latin: *munimentum fidei*; Hebrew: *Chizzuk Emunah*</note>—in which he took it upon himself to systematically attack the Christian truth (*)—

<note>(*) The famous Johann Christoph Wagenseil took care to have this pestilential book printed once again in Altdorf, Germany, in the year 1681 (joined with two others of equal malice but lesser strength) under the title *The Fiery Darts of Satan* <note>original: *tela ignea Sathanæ*</note>. This book is contained in Volume 2; as Wagenseil relates, he obtained the manuscript from a Jew in Ceuta, Africa, located across the Strait of Gibraltar, who was particularly pleased with its contents. It is my intention to read this great adversary of ours, if life and means suffice: *for it is* (to use the words of Wagenseil) *a monument greatly worthy that we Christians should exert our strength in its overthrow; since there exists no other work written by the Jews on this unhappy subject that more strongly confirms them in their errors and hinders the recognition of the truth.* May GOD grant that I am able to carry out what I have intended!</note>

had to be prolonged to exactly two days; the statute is recorded thus: **Capital cases are finished on the same day for acquittal, but on the following day for conviction. Therefore, they do not judge on either the eve of the Sabbath or the eve of a festival** <note>original Hebrew: דיני נפשות גומרין בו ביום לזכות . וביום שלאחריו לחובה . לפיכך אין דנין לא בערב שבת ולא בערב יום טוב</note>. The reasoning for this turns on the fact that, lest by the haste of a single day, those who were either able or desired to bring forward anything for the safety of the accused might be hindered. Therefore, this extra day added in favor of the defendant could in no way be twisted to his destruction; but it was so necessary to finish the trial on that second day that if the judges neglected even this, the accused could no longer be punished because of **the delay of justice** <note>original Hebrew: עינוי הדין (*Innu Ha-Din*), literally "the affliction of the law," referring to the suffering caused by an overlong trial</note> as they call it. <meta>This page continues the technical legal argument based on the Mishnah (Tractate Sanhedrin). The previous page established that while acquittals could be decided in one day, capital convictions required a second day of deliberation to ensure the defendant's rights were protected.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<columns>2</columns>

...rightly or wrongly this rule was introduced, I do not discuss; as long as the truth of the matter is clear. From this two-day restriction on trials, another rule that had to be observed necessarily followed: namely, that a capital trial could not be started on either the eve of a festival or the eve of the Sabbath. For, if the death penalty was to be imposed, it **was not permitted** to finish the trial on the same day, so that the defendant might have the respite of a day for his defense. On the Sabbath or a festival there was a <term>justitium</term> <note>A legal term for a period when courts are closed and formal legal proceedings are suspended</note>; therefore, the trial could not be concluded on those days, which would have served as the "second day" of the trial. Thus, there would be <term>Inu ha-Din</term> <note>Hebrew: עינוי הדין; the author translates this as "mora justitiae" or a **delay of justice**. In Jewish law, this refers to the "affliction of the legal process" where a delay in sentencing causes unnecessary suffering to the accused</note>, by which the defendant would be rightly withdrawn from punishment. With these foundations laid, let us turn to the most divine JESUS, who did not shrink from offering himself to a sacrilegious trial for our salvation. The hatred of the Jews leaves us no room for doubt that they exerted every force and cunning to ensure they 

<column-break/>

<page-num>215</page-num>
<sig>O 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: ex-</meta>

might take CHRIST away. It is certain, therefore, that they regarded the trial as a **capital case**; from which it follows that two days should have been spent by them to carry out their wicked work of darkness, lest by excessive greed and haste they might hinder the death of CHRIST, which they so intensely desired. But it is also most certain that they wanted to know the Savior was removed as quickly as possible, so much did they fear him. However, they had reached the final day before the festival; only that single day stood between the Sabbath and the feast of Passover. What should they do? Should they break the legal order of the trial? They feared that they might face rejection from Pilate—who was proving to be quite difficult regarding the condemnation of CHRIST—and that they would be openly convicted of injustice by the Lord's disciples. Therefore, they hurry and [seize] CHRIST in the dead of night

Having been taken into the <term>Sanhedrin</term> <note>original: synedrium; the supreme judicial and religious council of ancient Israel</note> first to Caiaphas, they then presented him to Pilate (for the Evangelists make distinct mention of both judgment halls) so that, when he was led by them to Pilate, they might persuade the Roman governor that they were carrying out every detail of their law to the letter. They claimed that this was the second day on which he was being held, and that according to the prescription of their law, a final judgment absolutely had to be imposed. By this reasoning, they also forced Pilate out of the opportunity for delay, using their festivals as a pretext—claiming that if he postponed the matter, it would create a legal obstacle to the punishment.

But was this to do violence to the words and to commit a fraud against the statute? Who indeed denies it? Let the opportunity be given: for I will weigh the judicial process exactly—not according to the Gospel, but according to the <term>Talmud</term> <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism containing the body of Jewish civil and ceremonial law</note> itself, which it builds upon. <page-num>218</page-num>
<meta>catchword: שהוא (which is)</meta>

<image-desc>A small decorative typographic ornament consisting of fleurons and asterisks is centered at the top of the page.</image-desc>

It is said (1) <note>original Hebrew: בחצות לילה אקום; Psalm 119:62</note> *at midnight I will rise*: *at midnight I arose to praise you* (or to confess); and in another place (2) <note>original Hebrew: קדמו עיני אשמורות; Psalm 119:148</note> *my eyes anticipated the watches*: if both are true—as they are—and there are *watches* <note>original Latin: vigiliæ (plural), rather than vigilia (singular)</note> at midnight, there must be at least two of them. Lest the following part of the night should also have two, then that moment would not truly be the middle of the night. Regarding the day, which follows the final watch of the night, the hours are counted by taking the start from the first number. For example: what is called the *third* hour in this system—since at the <term>equinox</term> <note>the time of year when day and night are of equal length</note> the start of the day is at the sixth hour—becomes our *ninth* hour. Similarly, their *sixth* hour becomes our *twelfth* (noon), with the numbers following one after another in succession. To support this matter, it is sufficient to cite (3) <note>original Hebrew: עד ג׳ שעות</note> *until the third hour*

---

(1) Psalm 119, verse 62. (2) same Psalm, verse 148.
(3) The *Tur Orach Chaim* <note>A foundational code of Jewish law</note> in the section regarding the *Kriat Shema* <note>The daily recitation of the "Shema" prayer</note>, Section 58, Paragraph 1.

<column-break/>

<page-num>219</page-num>
<meta>catchword: net</meta>

<image-desc>A small decorative typographic ornament consisting of fleurons and asterisks is centered at the top of the page.</image-desc>

<note>original Hebrew: שהוא רביעית היום</note> *which is the fourth part of the day*; this is the time for extending the morning prayers. Therefore, they begin to count at sunrise. Consequently, Matthew, Mark, and Luke—when they say that CHRIST was raised onto the cross at the *third* hour, and that darkness fell at the *sixth*—begin from the time it is customary to do so: namely, from the first hour of the day (which is our *sixth*). Thus, for them, at the ninth hour (according to our numbering), CHRIST is crucified; at the twelfth hour (noon), however, a darkness by the greatest of portents snatched away the sun from the middle of the sky. John does not follow this same method of calculation. Instead, as a way to silently reproach the Jews for their injustice—that they had condemned Christ to death in a single day, contrary to their own statute—he begins counting from the time the Savior was first brought before the High Priest. He therefore begins from the final watch of the night, which contains three hours...

[concluded]; then indeed it agrees exactly with the rest; for since he gained three hours (those in which the scribes were looking around for a witness and a cause by which they might overwhelm CHRIST), and since he contracted them into one with the day: what is the *third* hour for the others is rightly called the *sixth* for this one: and (I believe) all the conflict, which seemed to be present, has been (*) removed.

The lingering doubt that remains is that the Evangelists say: *and immediately in the morning holding a council etc.* and *day had dawned etc.* <note>Matthew 27:1 and Luke 22:66</note> which words seem to exclude the night watch and any calculation from it. ### CHAPTER IV.

*CHRIST suffered on that day, the evening of which was the beginning of the Passover for the Jews. Nevertheless, He rightly and legally ate the Passover with His disciples on the preceding day. A mystical rite is added, which is still in use among the Jews today.*

The matter regarding the <term>Passover</term> <note>The major Jewish spring festival (Pesach) commemorating the Exodus from Egypt.</note> celebrated by the Savior as He was about to suffer is not yet settled beyond controversy; and such a variety of opinions distracts consensus more than it determines it. (*) Therefore, the more 

---

(*) <note>original: "Originem hujus Controversiæ..."</note> Gravesonius presents the origin of this controversy in *Ecclesiastical History*, Vol. 4, collection 3, Venice edition, 1726; it is also found in Harduin in *Acts of the Councils*, Vol. 7, Paris Royal edition, 1714, folio 214, etc. Furthermore, the esteemed Gravesonius cites various opinions in his *Treatise on the Mysteries and Years of CHRIST*, Vol. 2, dissertation 19, starting from folio 39 of the same edition.

They unanimously contend that the <term>Feast of Unleavened Bread</term> was postponed by the Jews (or most of them) in that year by one day, and thus to the 15th; but was observed by CHRIST at the legal time: that is, on the second evening of the 14th; though they disagree on the reasons for the delay. Secondly, there is a statute by which the Jews were forbidden from beginning a trial—specifically one in which a person's life was at stake <note>This refers to capital cases, which carried the death penalty.</note>—on the eve of either the Sabbath or a feast day. We brought forward this statute in the previous chapter. The words state: "Therefore they do not judge either on the eve of the Sabbath or on the eve of a festival" <note>original Hebrew: לפיכך אין דנין לא בערב שבת ולא בערב יום טוב</note>. The meaning is this: *therefore, judgment is not rendered on the eve of the Sabbath, nor of the feasts.* This is because a sentence of condemnation had to be entirely concluded on the following day, as was set forth in the wording of the statute. And indeed, if it had been permitted to carry out a sentence on the Sabbath or a feast day, this clause would be useless; for it is for this very reason that the eves of feasts are declared unsuitable for legal business: so that a **delay of justice** <note>original Hebrew: ענוי הדין (Inui ha-din); original Latin: "mora justitiæ"</note> would not be created, by which the guilty party might be withdrawn from punishment. For in that case, there would be no "delay," as the trial would be finished on the second day, just as the law prescribes. Truly, these facts clearly object to the theory, so that those who affirm that Christ was driven to the cross on the first and most solemn day

<page-num>229</page-num>

of the feast are striving in vain. This is exactly what would have happened, since Pilate, who was anxiously looking for every opportunity to release him, would have seized upon such a legal error with both hands. Furthermore, the people themselves would have disapproved of this lawless way of proceeding; from that, they would not only have guessed at Christ's innocence, but would have moved to protect it. Truly, the Pharisees were far too clever to open such a wide door of escape for Christ! Hence that saying: *Not during the feast, lest there be a riot among the people* (1). Therefore

The tenor of the command regarding the eating of the Passover lamb, which **GOD** imparted to Moses and Aaron, is this: (1) *“And you shall keep it until the fourteenth day of this month, and the whole assembly of the congregation of Israel shall slaughter it between the evenings”* <note>original Hebrew: "והיה לכם למשמרת עד ארבעה עשר יום לחדש הזה ושחטו אתו כל קהל עדת ישראל בין הערבים"</note>. (1) Exodus chapter 12, verse 6.

*teenth day of this month*, and *the whole congregation of Israel shall sacrifice it* (the Passover lamb) *between the evenings*. Upon this very comma, the opinions of the ancient Rabbis are divided into two parts. A dispute has arisen from the phrase <term>between the evenings</term> <note>original Hebrew: *בין ערבים* (*bein ha-arbayim*). This refers to the specific window of time mandated for the sacrifice, which remains a point of liturgical debate.</note>. Rabbi Joshua defines this time as being between half-past one in the afternoon and the second evening of the 14th day. In opposition to him, Ben Bethera takes the <term>two evenings</term> (and indeed the Hebrew word is in the dual form) to mean the *first* evening of the 14th day and the *second* evening, which is the legal start of the 15th. The <term>Tractate Zebachim</term> (1) <note>A volume of the Talmud dealing with the laws of animal sacrifices.</note> recounts the foundations upon which both opinions rest in a long series, which do not strictly pertain to our concerns; however, this must not be overlooked: in the Talmud, neither of these opinions is rejected or accepted; this very free-

...it leaves it open to anyone to embrace whichever view they prefer (I wish to mention this in passing), as is permitted in all matters of opinion where the Rabbis disagree and nothing is defined as to which of the two views must be upheld. The <term>rule for deciding</term> <note>original Latin: *formula decidendi*</note> such matters is: *the law follows the majority against the individual* <note>original Hebrew: יחיד ורבים הלכה כרבים</note>. For example: *Rabbi So-and-so says this, but the Sages say that; the law follows the Sages* <note>original Hebrew: כמו ר' פלוני אומר כך וחכמים אומרים כך הלכה כחכמים</note>. (This means: *if one person thinks differently than the many, the ruling is according to the many; for instance: R. N. said this, but the wise men said that: the ruling follows the many*). 

Therefore, if the opinion of <term>Ben Bethera</term> <note>The "Sons of Bethera" were a family of legal authorities in the late Second Temple period who famously deferred to Hillel the Elder on matters of Passover law.</note> stands—and it does stand, as has been stated—then the <term>first evening</term> <note>The author refers to the start of the 14th of Nisan (Thursday evening), as opposed to the "second evening" at the end of the 14th (Friday afternoon).</note> of the fourteenth moon is valid for sacrificing the Paschal lamb. And if it is valid, then our **Savior** legitimately ate the lamb with his twelve disciples. By this reasoning, He both satisfied the requirements of the Law—by approving of this alternative opinion and conforming Himself to it—and yet was still able, truly before the official feast on the 14th, 

<meta>catchword: work</meta>

<column-break/>

# Page 237

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>❧ *** ❧</header>

to complete the work of redemption.

However, there are two matters we must clear up that might seem to stand in the way of this adopted opinion. First, while it might be permitted (as we argue) to sacrifice the lamb on the 13th day or during the first evening of the 14th, eating it might not be permitted; for the text says: (1) *And they shall eat the flesh in that night* <note>original Hebrew: ואכלו את הבשר בלילה הזה</note>—meaning the night of the fourteenth. This is certainly true; the text says *in that night*, but it does not specify the *second* evening of the fourteenth. Therefore, if the opinion of Ben Bethera is admitted, this difficulty vanishes, especially when that other commandment is called to mind: (2) *You shall let nothing of it remain until morning, and whatever remains of it until morning you shall burn with fire* <note>original Hebrew: ולא תותירו ממנו עד בקר והנותר ממנו באש תשרפו</note>. (That is: *you shall not leave anything of it until morning*).

<meta>catchword: &</meta>

...[father of the] family reclines with the whole assembly of his people: and he enters into the rite of the Sanctification of the cup, which they call *Kadesh* <note>original: קדש; the first step of the Passover Seder, meaning "Sanctification"</note>. The head of the household performs all the ceremonies, including this one: a plate is present covered with a cloth folded lengthwise; into this, three loaves of unleavened bread <term>azymes: bread made without yeast, known as Matzah</term> are placed, each of which has its own name. <page-num>253</page-num>

...mity lies in the dish: the long part is extended outside of it; on this cloth at the end which is inside the dish, *Israel* is placed; after this, it is covered by the cloth being folded back over it; on top of the covering of the first, another is placed, whose name is *Levi* <note>original: לוי</note>: which is again hidden by the cloth being bent back. The top one, which is added, is *Cohen* <note>original: כהן; "Priest"</note> to them, which they cover just like the others. Therefore, between each of the unleavened loaves there is a layer of that cloth, with a final covering on the top. He then takes out the middle of these unleavened loaves and breaks it into equal parts—namely, the *Levi*: one part he hides under the cushion (for the head of the household sits upon a couch) and it remains hidden while the supper lasts; just as will be described shortly below, and from this it becomes the *Afikoman* <note>original: אפיקומן; a piece of Matzah broken off and hidden early in the Seder, to be eaten at the end</note>, as it is named. ...[and they open the door] as wide as possible; they do this as a sign of full security, as those who are less foolish explain it. It must not be overlooked that he eats a portion of the toasted unleavened bread <term>azyme: bread made without yeast</term> with a bitter herb <note>original: "amara herba"; likely maror, such as horseradish or romaine lettuce</note>, and offers it to be eaten; thus he celebrates the splendors of the festive meal. After the meal, he eats a portion of that hidden unleavened bread <note>original: "occulati azymi"</note>: and suddenly seizing the cup, he says a blessing and drinks; after which it is not permitted for him to eat anything further that evening. From the remainder of the *Afikoman* <note>original: "Aphi Cuman"; the piece of matzah set aside to be eaten at the end of the meal</note>, a small piece is broken off by each person and sewn with religious devotion into the *four corners* <note>original: "ארבע כנפות" (Arba Kanfot); refers to the four-cornered fringed garment worn by observant Jewish men</note> of their garments, which they keep until the year has come full circle and they replace it with a new one. ...provided for in many matters and protected from dangers. (1)
Should anyone ask a Jew first about the name and the meaning of this *Afikoman* <note>original: "אפיקומן"</note>, he will say that this word expresses some *dessert* <note>original: "bellarium"</note> or after-dinner delicacy. What is the derivation of the word? He does not know. From what time should the beginning of this rite be traced? He is ignorant, except that it is ancient.
What is the cause for doing this? Some assign it to the fact that the middle bread denotes the shortening of the *Egyptian servitude*, which was reduced from the four hundred years announced to Abraham to two hundred and ten years. Others say it refers to the *subjugation to the kingdoms* <note>original: "שעבוד מלכיות" (Shibud malkhuyot)</note>, that is, the time of the servitude which they served under the Babylonians and others for two hundred years...

Regarding the <term>etymology: the origin of a word</term> <note>original: "ἔτυμον"</note> of the name: from where does the meaning of "dessert" <note>original: "bellarii"</note> come? From the root? Furthermore, even the rabbis themselves do not admit that the years of Egyptian servitude were halved; instead, they begin the calculation from that time when it was first made known to Abraham,

<page-num>257</page-num>
<sig>R</sig>

and by a calculated reckoning of the fates, they complete the number of four hundred years. How is it certain to them, or from where does the evidence of the reduction of the second servitude come? From scripture? Truly nothing. And I fear that they have experienced, and will further experience, far too many years in this final servitude of theirs which never seems to end; and the addition of two centuries is minimal compared to such a long duration.

Let me be permitted to look upon this ritual with a **Christian eye**; for I too agree that it is very ancient and an intimation of liberation, which indeed lies hidden within it. The middle unleavened bread indicates the tribe of the same name, *Levi*, which was indeed chosen for worship and the priesthood, but not for the fullness of these; this seems to me to be denoted by the other portion hidden under the pillow: that a halved power is separated until it is filled by the entry of the **fullness of the nations**.

# CHAPTER V.

## The reason for the prohibition of leaven based on an analysis of the texts of Exodus, chapter 12, verses 34 and 35.

It is beyond dispute that nothing was done or commanded at random anywhere, whether it concerned a symbolic figure or the sacred ceremonies. But just as the reasons for many things escape us even today, so there are certain explanations that provoke us logically <note>original Greek: κατά λόγον (*kata logon*), meaning according to reason or proportion</note> but do not fully satisfy. To this belongs entirely the prohibition of leaven <term>leaven: an agent like yeast used to make bread rise, which was strictly forbidden during Passover to symbolize the haste of the Israelites' departure from Egypt</term> during the time of Passover. For they are commanded to banish it and to abstain for seven continuous days; and to abstain under threat of death for whoever does otherwise: (1) *“whoever eats what is leavened, that soul shall be cut off from Israel: from the first day until the seventh day;”*

This very doctrine <note>original Greek: δόγμα (dogma)</note> of faith creates trouble for the Jews, and they, in turn, for us: since they cannot grasp in their thinking how it could be possible for the **MESSIAH** to be born without a father? Should he be born without a father, and yet trace his lineage from the tribe of Judah? For the lineage and origin are counted according to the Father. Thus, they conclude: **CHRIST**

<column-break/>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<meta>catchword: As</meta>

(according to what we say) is born without a Father; therefore, he is without a family, which is named through males, not females: he who is without a family is not the **MESSIAH**, since he was to be a member of the same tribe as Judah. This reasoning is not so forceful and is easily overturned; they build upon the hypothesis (which they meanwhile disguise by not conceding it) that **CHRIST** was born without a Father; therefore, let us also hold it as if demonstrated. If **CHRIST** was born without a Father, and yet was born, he was a man; if he was a man, and the origin of this man must be defined, of what lineage would you say he is? The father's? There is none. Therefore, it is of the one who exists, namely the mother. There is indeed a calculation of origin in the usual order according to the father, but that is where a father exists; but where there is that supernatural event, such that offspring is begotten with no father, there this calculation must cease entirely.

The author is refuting Jewish commentators who interpret Psalm 72 as a song for King Solomon, arguing instead that the text implies a pre-existent figure—the Messiah—who existed before the creation of the sun.</meta>

<page-num>272</page-num>
<header>❦ ⁂ ❦</header>

(1) Therefore, he can be accounted for under this name of the **MESSIAH**; but by no means as Solomon. For how could *David* proclaim concerning him: *Before the sun his name shall be propagated like a son?* <note>original Hebrew: "lipnei shemesh yinnin shemo." The author uses the Latin "filiabitur" (to be made a son) to translate the rare Hebrew verb "yinnin," connecting the Messiah's name to the word for "offspring" or "son."</note> Could this be asserted of anyone other than the **MESSIAH**, of whom Micah also says: (2) *And his goings forth are from of old, from the days of eternity?* <note>original Hebrew: "u-motsa’otav mi-kedem mi-yemei olam." A reference to Micah 5:2, traditionally used by Christian scholars to prove the Messiah's eternal pre-existence.</note> Finally, let these more recent authors yield to the authority of the ancients; those ancients who believed that this psalm should be referred to the **MESSIAH** without any ambiguity. It is enough to call forth one witness. Rabbi Eliezer says: (3) *Before the world was created, the name of the Messiah was Yinon, as it is said: "Before the sun his name shall flourish." And another scripture says: "And you, Bethlehem Ephrata, etc., his goings forth are from of old..."*

*...from of old, before the world was created.* <note>original Hebrew: "ad she-lo nivra ha-olam."</note> *How do we know the name of the* **MESSIAH** *already existed when the world was not yet created? Because it is said: "Before the sun his name shall sprout." Elsewhere it says: "But you, Bethlehem Ephrata, etc., and his name is from the beginning"—that is: when the world had not yet been created.* Therefore, by the testimony of his own people—specifically those who hold greater authority than him—we refute Jarchius <note>original: "Jarchium," a Latinized name for Rashi (Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki), the famous 11th-century commentator. The author is criticizing Rashi's more literal/historical interpretations.</note>. He interprets as referring to Solomon what the ancients rightly applied to the **MESSIAH**. He also distorts the word *before* <note>original Hebrew: "lipnei."</note> (as both Eliezer and all others understand it, and as the very nature of the language demands it be understood). He, I say, limits this to *all the days of the sun* <note>original Hebrew: "kol yemei ha-shemesh"</note> and thus *in the presence of the sun*. However, the Chaldean <note>A common term for the Targum, the ancient Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible.</note> is on our side against him, translating it as *before the sun existed.* <note>original Aramaic: "kodam ma-yei shemsha."</note> Thus, it is settled and defined that this psalm is to be attributed to the **MESSIAH**—

<summary>The author argues that Psalm 72 and Micah 5:2 refer to the Messiah’s eternal pre-existence rather than to King Solomon. He uses ancient Jewish sources, such as Rabbi Eliezer and the Targum, to refute the medieval commentator Rashi’s interpretation.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument regarding the divine and eternal nature of the Messiah, specifically analyzing Psalm 72:17. 274

it was also said of him: *His name shall endure forever; his name shall be propagated as a son before the sun.* <note>original Hebrew: "yehi shemo l'olam lipnei shemesh yanin shemo"</note> The Masoretes <term>Masoretes|Jewish scholars between the 6th and 10th centuries who standardized the Hebrew Bible text</term> note that for the word *yanin* <note>original: יָנִין</note>, it should be read as *Yinnon* <note>original: יִנּוֹן</note>. The origin of this word is from *nin* <note>original: נִין</note>, which means "son"; hence the sense of the text is, *before the sun his name is son*. The Rabbis also determined that the future name of the **MESSIAH** would be *Yinnon*. (1) : *What is the name of the MESSIAH?* The school of Janai <note>Rabbi Yannai was a prominent first-generation Amoraic sage in Israel</note> replied: *Yinnon is his name, as it is said: "Before the sun his name shall be Yinnon."* Thus, the meaning of the cited verse is: *before the sun the name of the MESSIAH is "son."* Tell me, what is so remarkable about the title *son*? It is remarkable indeed, if he is the *son before the sun*.

For a *son* does not exist without a Father; and who is the Father before the sun? A man? Would they not dare to say so? Therefore, he is **GOD**. Rightly, then, in the following verses, a blessing to the Most High is thundered forth (1) *...before he was "son" his name was: Blessed be the Lord GOD, the GOD of Israel, who alone does wondrous things: Blessed be the name of his GLORY forever, and all the earth shall be filled with his GLORY: amen! amen!* <note>The author provides a combined translation of the Hebrew from Psalm 72:17-19</note> It is a wonder indeed, possible only for **GOD**, to be both a son and before the sun, and the *son of David*. Thus also Isaiah (2) proclaims the **MESSIAH** with this title: *For a child is born to us, a son is given to us.* <note>original Hebrew: "ki yeled yullad lanu ben nittan lanu," from Isaiah 9:6</note>
